ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE IN WORKERS' PARTICIPATION IN
MANAGEMENT: OPPORTUNITIES, RISKS AND THE WAY FORWARD
R. UVASRI
 3rd Year BBA, Sriram College of Arts and Science
Abstract
Workers' Participation in Management (WPM) has been a cornerstone of India's industrial
relations policy since the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 introduced Works Committees, and
Article 43A of the Constitution (inserted by the 42nd Amendment, 1976) directed the State to
secure workers' participation in the management of industries. In practice, however,
participation has remained uneven, largely formal and concentrated in the organized sector.
The rapid entry of Artificial Intelligence (AI) into human resource management, workplace
monitoring, performance evaluation and decision -making now reshape both the possibilities
and the threats to meaningful participation.  This conceptual paper reviews Indian and
international literature to examine how AI can strengthen or weaken workers' participation in
management. Using a structured narrative review of secondary sources (textbooks, journal
articles, policy documents and statutes), the paper identifies four themes:
(i) AI as an enabler of employee voice, information sharing and grievance redressal;
(ii) algorithmic management and surveillance as threats to autonomy and collective
bargaining;
(iii) structural gaps in the Indian legal and institutional framework; and
(iv) the need to treat AI adoption itself as a subject of participation.
The paper proposes a participation -centered framework for AI adoption and offers practical
suggestions for employers, trade unions and policymakers. It concludes that AI will s upport
genuine participation only when workers have a say in how it is designed, deployed and
governed.
Keywords: Workers' Participation in Management; Artificial Intelligence; Employee Voice;
Algorithmic Management; Industrial Relations; Industrial Democracy; India
1. Introduction
Industrial democracy, the idea that those who work in an enterprise should have a voice in
decisions affecting them, has long been a stated goal of Indian labor policy. The Constitution,
the Five-Year Plans, the Joint Management Councils scheme of 1958, the 1975 scheme of
Shop Councils and Joint Councils, and the appointment of worker -directors in public sector
undertakings all reflect this aspiration (Mamoria, Mamoria & Gankar, 2015; Sinha, Sinha &
Shekhar, 2017; Venkata Ratnam, 2006). Despite this, scholars have repeatedly observed that
participation in India has been limited in scope, weakly institutionalized and often dependent
on management's goodwill (Monappa, Nambudiri & Selvaraj, 2012; Ramaswamy, 1988).

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 213

Meanwhile, Industry 4.0 technologies are transforming Indian workplaces. AI -enabled tools
now screen résumés, predict attrition, allocate shifts, monitor productivity, power HR
chatbots and analyze employee sentiment (Sivathanu & Pillai, 2018; Rana & Sharm a, 2019;
Bhardwaj, Singh & Kumar, 2020). NITI Aayog's national strategy positions AI as a driver of
growth and inclusion (NITI Aayog, 2018), and its later approach document stresses
transparency, accountability and human oversight (NITI Aayog, 2021). This creates a double-
edged situation. AI could give workers better information and easier channels to raise
concerns. It could equally give management unprecedented control through opaque, data -
driven systems that workers cannot question. This paper asks: how can AI be integrated into
Indian workplaces so that it deepens, rather than dilutes, workers' participation in
management?
2. Review of Literature
2.1 Workers' participation in management in India
Indian textbooks and studies define WPM as the involvement of workers, through their
representatives, in the decision -making process of an organization, ranging from information
sharing and consultation to co -determination and self -management (Mamoria et al., 2015;
Sinha et al., 2017; Srivastava, 2016). Commonly discussed forms in India include:
 Works Committees (Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, now provided for under the
Industrial Relations Code, 2020)
 Joint Management Councils and Shop/Joint Councils
 Worker-directors on boards of public sector undertakings
 Bipartite forums, safety committees and grievance committees
 Quality circles, suggestion schemes and employee stock ownership plans
Pylee and George (2013) and Venkata Ratnam (2006) note that most of these bodies operate
at the level of information sharing and co nsultation, with limited influence over strategic
decisions. Ramaswamy (1988) argues that participation succeeds only where unions and
management share a minimum level of trust. Aswathappa (2013) and Rao (2012) emphasize
that participation improves morale, productivity and industrial peace when it is genuine rather
than symbolic.
2.2 Artificial intelligence in human resource management
Indian and international scholars document rapid AI adoption across the HR function:
recruitment, onboarding, learning and development, performance management, employee
engagement and workforce analytics (Bhardwaj et al., 2020; Rana & Sharma, 2019;
Sivathanu & Pillai, 2018). Budhwar, Malik, De Silva and Thevisuthan (2022) review the
challenges and opportunities of AI in intern ational HRM and highlight concerns about bias,
ethics and employee trust. Malik, Budhwar and Kazmi (2023) propose a strategic framework
for AI -assisted HRM, stressing that outcomes depend on how AI is embedded in
organizational strategy and employee relati ons, not on the technology alone. Dessler and

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 214

Varkkey (2011) and Aswathappa (2013) similarly treat technology as an enabler whose
impact depends on HR philosophy.
2.3 AI, employee voice and participation
Studies on employee engagement in Indian organizatio ns show that communication,
involvement and trust are central to engagement (Bhatnagar, 2007). AI tools such as
sentiment analysis, chatbots and digital suggestion platforms can widen the channels through
which workers express opinions. However, the litera ture also warns that algorithmic
monitoring and automated decision -making may reduce autonomy, increase stress and
weaken collective voice when workers cannot see or challenge the logic behind decisions
(Budhwar et al., 2022; Malik et al., 2023). Policy do cuments recognize this risk: NITI Aayog
(2021) lists transparency, accountability, fairness and protection of privacy as core principles
of responsible AI, while the Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 introduces obligations
on the processing of personal data, including employee data.
2.4 Research gap
Indian literature on WPM is mostly pre -digital and institutional, while Indian literature on AI
in HR focuses on efficiency, recruitment and analytics. Very few works link the two by
asking how AI affect s the institutions and processes of participation such as works
committees, joint councils and collective bargaining. This paper addresses that gap.
3. Objectives
1. To examine the concept, forms and status of WPM in India.
2. To analyze how AI applications can support workers' participation.
3. To identify the risks AI poses to participation and industrial relations.
4. To suggest a framework and practical measures for participation-centered AI adoption.
4. Research Methodology
 Nature of study: Conceptual and descriptive; qualitative.
 Design: Structured narrative review with thematic synthesis.
 Sources: Secondary data only: standard Indian textbooks on industrial relations and
HRM, peer -reviewed journal articles on AI in HRM and employee engagement,
government and policy documents (NITI Aayog reports, the Industrial Relations Code,
2020, and the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023) and the constitutional provision
on workers' participation.
 Selection criteria: Works were included if they addressed (a)  workers' participation or
industrial democracy in India, or (b) AI/digital technology in HR and employee relations.
Priority was given to Indian authors and Indian institutional contexts.
 Analysis: Content from the sources was coded and grouped into recur ring themes, which
form the findings below.
 Limitation: As the study relies on secondary sources, the findings are indicative and
propositional, not statistically generalized.

Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 215

5.1 AI can widen access to information and voice
Participation at even its basic  level depends on workers having timely, understandable
information. AI -enabled dashboards, multilingual chatbots and mobile platforms can share
information on production targets, safety data, wages and benefits, and can allow workers to
submit grievances or suggestions with anonymity. Sentiment analysis of feedback may help
work committees identify recurring concerns early. In a linguistically diverse country,
language-processing tools can lower barriers for workers who are less comfortable with
English or written communication.
5.2 AI can improve the quality of participative decisions
Predictive analytics on absenteeism, accidents, attrition and skill needs can give joint forums
an evidence base for discussions on safety, training and workload. When such d ata is shared
with worker representatives, participation moves from opinion -based bargaining towards
fact-based dialogue, which the literature associates with higher trust and better outcomes
(Ramaswamy, 1988; Aswathappa, 2013).
5.3 Algorithmic management may undermine participation
The same technologies can be used to monitor keystrokes, movements and output in real
time, to allocate work and to rate performance automatically. Where workers do not know
what data is collected or how scores are computed, par ticipation becomes hollow. Concerns
identified in the literature include opacity, embedded bias, intensification of work, stress and
reduced discretion (Budhwar et al., 2022; Malik et al., 2023). Such systems may also
individualize the employment relations hip and thereby weaken the collective identity on
which unions and works committees rest.
5.4 Institutional and legal gaps in India
Existing participative structures were designed for a pre -digital workplace. Works
committees and joint councils rarely have  a mandate over technology decisions. The
Industrial Relations Code, 2020 retains committee -based mechanisms but does not
specifically address algorithmic decision -making. The Digital Personal Data Protection Act,
2023 protects personal data but does not c reate a collective voice for workers in how AI
systems are designed. Further, participation in India is largely confined to the organized
sector (Venkata Ratnam, 2006; Sinha et al., 2017), so gig, platform and contract workers,
among the most exposed to algorithmic control, often lack any formal participative channel.
5.5 Skills, trust and readiness are decisive
Successful participation requires workers and union leaders who understand what AI does.
Without digital literacy, worker representatives cannot qu estion or negotiate AI -related
decisions. The literature on Indian HR practice repeatedly links trust and communication to
successful change (Bhatnagar, 2007; Rao, 2012), suggesting that consultation before AI
deployment is a precondition for acceptance.

6. Suggestions
1. Make AI an agenda item in participative forums. Works Committees, Joint Management
Councils and safety committees should discuss AI adoption, its purpose, data collected and
impact on jobs before implementation.
2. Create joint AI review committees. Include worker representatives in evaluating AI tools
used for hiring, appraisal, discipline and surveillance.
3. Ensure transparency and explainability. Workers should be told in plain language what
data is collected and how automated scores or decisions are produced.
4. Keep humans in the loop. Decisions on termination, disciplina ry action, promotion and
wages should not rest solely on algorithmic output, and workers should have a right to seek
human review.
5. Comply with data protection norms. Employers should follow the Digital Personal Data
Protection Act 2023, limit data colle ction to legitimate purposes and involve worker
representatives in setting workplace data policies.
6. Invest in digital skills for workers and union leaders. Joint training programmes on AI
literacy and reskilling can enable informed participation and ease transition concerns.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 217

7. Use AI to widen voice. Deploy anonymous, multilingual, mobile -friendly feedback and
grievance tools, and close the loop by publishing action taken.
8. Extend participation to non -standard workers. Organizations and platforms shoul d create
consultation channels for contract and platform workers.
9. Policy support. The Ministry of Labor and Employment could issue guidelines linking the
Industrial Relations Code with responsible -AI principles already articulated by NITI Aayog
(2021), including consultation rights on workplace technology.
10. Pilot and evaluation. Begin with pilot projects in selected units, review outcomes jointly
and scale up only after building trust.
7. Limitations and Scope for Future Research
This study is based o n secondary sources and does not include primary data. Future research
may use surveys and interviews with managers, union leaders and workers in manufacturing,
IT/ITeS, banking and platform work to test the proposed framework empirically, compare
sectors and states, and examine the effect of AI -enabled participation on productivity, job
satisfaction and industrial harmony.
8. Conclusion
AI is neither inherently a friend nor an enemy of workers' participation in management. Used
transparently and jointly go verned, it can supply better information, faster channels of voice
and more evidence-based dialogue in works committees and joint councils. Used as a closed
tool of surveillance and control, it can hollow out even the limited participation that exists in
Indian industry. The deciding factor is participation in AI itself: when workers and their
representatives have a say in why AI is introduced, what data it uses and how its outputs are
applied, technology strengthens industrial democracy. For India, where t he constitutional
vision of participation has long outpaced practice, the AI era offers both a risk and an
opportunity to finally make participation real.

A STUDY ON BRAND MANAGEMENT AND RURAL MARKETING
OPPORTUNITIES IN INDIA
Mrs. S. Nagaveni
Assistant Professor, Department of Commerce,
 Sriram College of Arts and Science, Perumalpattu-602024.
ABSTRACT
Rural markets play an important role in the growth of the Indian economy and offer
significant opportunities for businesses. This study focuses on brand management and rural
marketing opportunities in India, with part icular attention to changing rural consumer
behavior, brand awareness, and purchasing patterns.
 Effective branding,
 affordable pricing,
 suitable distribution,
 and digital marketing can help companies reach rural consumers and build brand loyalty.
The study also highlights the major challenges and opportunities in rural markets. It
concludes that innovative and consumer -oriented brand management strategies can help
businesses achieve sustainable growth in rural India.
Keywords
Brand Management, Rural Marketing, Rural Consumers, Brand Awareness, Brand Loyalty,
Digital Marketing.
INTRODUCTION
Introduction   Fast-Moving Consumer Goods (FMCG) constitute one of the large st sectors of
the Indian economy, driven by widespread consumption patterns across both urban and rural
areas. Rural markets have emerged as a significant growth avenue for FMCG companies,
driven by increasing disposable incomes, improved infrastructure, a nd greater accessibility to
consumer goods in the present competitive business environment. Brand management and
marketing have become important components of business success. A brand represents more
than a name, logo, or product; it creates an identity, communicates value, builds customer
confidence, and develops a long -term relationship between the organization and its
customers. India is a diverse country with a large rural population and a significant number of
villages spread across different geograph ical, cultural, and economic regions. Rural markets
therefore represent an important area of opportunity for businesses. Changes in rural income,
education, infrastructure, communication facilities, digital connectivity, consumer awareness,
and access to f inancial services have gradually influenced rural consumers and their
purchasing behavior. Rural consumers are increasingly exposed to national and regional
brands through television, mobile phones, social media, e -commerce, and other digital
platforms. Rural marketing refers to the marketing of products and services to consumers
living in rural areas. It includes understanding rural consumer needs, developing suitable

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 220

products, establishing appropriate pricing strategies, creating effective communication, and
ensuring efficient distribution. Unlike urban markets, rural markets may have different
consumption patterns, income levels, cultural preferences, infrastructure facilities, and
purchasing behavior. Therefore, companies need to develop marketing strate gies that are
appropriate to the specific characteristics of rural consumers.
Rural India offers opportunities across several sectors, including consumer goods,
agriculture, banking, insurance, telecommunications, healthcare, education, automobiles,
retail, and digital services. However, companies operating in rural markets also face
challenges such as geographical dispersion, transportation and distribution difficulties,
seasonal income patterns, limited infrastructure in some areas, differences in languag e and
culture, and variations in consumer awareness. Therefore, effective brand management
combined with a well -planned rural marketing strategy is essential for organizations seeking
sustainable growth in rural India. Understanding rural consumers, adapti ng products and
communication to local needs, strengthening distribution networks, using digital
technologies, and building long -term consumer trust can help businesses explore the
opportunities available in rural markets.
BACKGROUND OF THE STUDY
Background of the present study, “Brand Management and Rural Marketing
Opportunities in India,” focuses on the growing importance of rural markets and the role of
effective brand management in addressing the changing needs of rural consumers. The study
seeks to und erstand the opportunities available to businesses, the factors influencing rural
consumer behavior, the importance of brand awareness and trust, and the challenges involved
in developing and implementing effective rural marketing strategies. Understanding these
developments is important for organizations seeking sustainable market expansion. A better
understanding of rural consumers and their changing expectations can help businesses
develop relevant brands, improve market reach, strengthen consumer relatio nships, and
identify emerging opportunities in the Indian rural market.

RURAL MARKETING OPPORTUNITIES IN INDIA
Rural marketing refers to marketing products and services  to consumers living in rural areas.
India has a large rural population, making rural markets an important opportunity for
businesses.
Major Opportunities
 Large Consumer Market
Rural India provides a large customer base for consumer goods and services.
 Growing Purchasing Power
Rising agricultural income, government schemes, employment opportunities, and
improved connectivity can increase rural purchasing power.
 Demand for FMCG Products
There is strong potential for products such as soaps, shampoos, packaged foods,
beverages, and household products.
 Agricultural Products and Services
Farmers need seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, agricultural equipment, irrigation products, and
technology-based farming solutions.

 Digital Marketing
Increasing smartphone and internet usage creates opportunities for social media, online

 Banking and Financial Services
Rural areas offer opportunities for banking, insurance, microfinance, and digital financial
services.
 Healthcare and Education
There is growing demand for affordable healthcare services, medicines, educational

 Improved Infrastructure
Better roads, electricity , telecommunications, and transportation make it easier for

NATURE OF THE STUDY
 Dynamic Consumer Behavior It examines how rural consumer preferences shift from
unbranded commodities to value -driven branded goods, empha sizing trust, utility, and
cultural resonance over sheer lifestyle appeal.
 Adaptation of the Marketing Mix The study analyzes the transition from traditional
urban (Product, Price, Place, Promotion) to the rural -focused Affordability, Availability,
Awareness, and Acceptability.
 Penetration and Sachet Revolution  highlights product and packaging innovation such
as single-use sachets and low-unit-cost packs that lower entry barriers for low-disposable-
income households.
 Socio-Economic Evolution captures the impact of rising rural literacy, digital and mobile
penetration, and governmental financial inclusion initiatives on consumer brand loyalty.
SCOPE OF STUDY
The growth of digital technology has also created new opportunities for rural
marketing in India. Mobile connectivity, digital payments, social media, online marketplaces,
and digital advertising have provided companies with new ways of reaching rural consumers.
At the same time, traditional methods such as local retailers, village -level p romotions,
demonstrations, community events, and word -of-mouth communication continue to have an
important role in many rural markets
FACTORS INFLUENCING BRAND MANAGEMENT
 Income Patterns Rural earnings depend heavily on agriculture. Buying peaks during
harvest seasons when cash flow is high.
 Cultural Values  Traditions, community norms, and religious beliefs strongly
influence brand loyalty. Virtue and social bonds matter more than flashy trends.
 Linguistic Diversity  India has many regional languages and d ialects.
Advertisements must use local languages to connect with buyers.
 Growing Aspirations As detailed in Rural Markets and Factors , better technology
and telecommunication give rural consumers the desire for higher -quality, modern
goods.

This study “Brand Management and Rural Marketing Opportunities in India,”

strengthen their brands and expand their presence in the growing rural ma rket. Hence the
Effective brand management enables companies to differentiate their products from

ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE IN PERFORMANCE APPRAISAL:
OPPORTUNITIES, CHALLENGES AND THE WAY FORWARD FOR INDIAN
ORGANISATIONS
S. Sathish Kumar
, III BBA, Sriram College of Arts and science
ABSTRACT
Performance appraisal remains one of the most consequential and most contested human
resource practices in Indian organisations. Traditional appraisal systems are widely criticised
for rather bias, subjectivity, infrequent feedback and a weak link with emp loyee
development. The rapid diffusion of artificial intelligence (AI), including machine learning,
natural language processing and people analytics, promises appraisal that is more data-driven,
continuous and consistent. This paper reviews the scholarly l iterature on AI in human
resource management (HRM) with a specific focus on performance appraisal, giving
emphasis to contributions by Indian authors and to Indian organisational contexts. Adopting a
descriptive, qualitative and secondary -data-based method ology, the paper undertakes a
thematic synthesis of peer -reviewed articles, books and policy documents. The review
identifies six themes: (i) expanding applications of AI across the appraisal cycle; (ii) the
potential for greater objectivity alongside the risk of algorithmic bias; (iii) the shift from
annual reviews to continuous feedback; (iv) employee perceptions of fairness, transparency
and trust; (v) ethical, privacy and legal concerns; and (vi) organisational readiness and
managerial capability. The p aper concludes that AI is best positioned as an augmenting tool
within a human -in-the-loop appraisal system rather than as a replacement for managerial
judgement, and it offers practical suggestions for HR leaders, policymakers and researchers.
Keywords: Artificial Intelligence; Performance Appraisal; Performance Management;
Human Resource Management; Algorithmic Fairness; People Analytics; India
1. INTRODUCTION
Performance appraisal is the systematic process of evaluating employees’ job performance,
and often their potential, against agreed standards so that organisations can take informed
decisions on pay, promotion, training and retention (Rao, 2004; Aswathappa , 2013; Dessler
& Varkkey, 2015). In the Indian corporate context, where organisations compete for scarce
talent and manage large, diverse and geographically dispersed workforces, appraisal has
become a strategic instrument of performance management and no t merely an annual
administrative ritual (Rao, 2004; Kandula, 2013).
Despite decades of refinement, conventional appraisal systems continue to attract criticism.
Common concerns include rater errors such as halo, leniency, central tendency and recency
effects; subjectivity in the assessment of behaviours and competencies; the infrequency of
annual reviews; the time burden on managers; and a weak link between appraisal outcomes
and employee development (Bhattacharyya, 2011; Kandula, 2013; DeNisi & Murphy, 20 17).
These weaknesses reduce perceived fairness, and perceived unfairness in turn erodes trust,
engagement and motivation. Artificial intelligence (AI), understood here as computer systems

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 225

that use techniques such as machine learning, natural language proc essing and predictive
analytics to perform tasks that ordinarily require human judgement, is increasingly embedded
in HR processes (Sivathanu & Pillai, 2018; Rana & Sharma, 2019; Dwivedi et al., 2021).
India’s national policy discourse on AI, including NITI Aayog’s (2018) national strategy, and
the enactment of the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 have made both the promise
and the risks of data-driven people decisions more visible to Indian employers.
Much of the Indian scholarship on AI in HRM h as concentrated on recruitment and talent
acquisition (Upadhyay & Khandelwal, 2018; Pillai & Sivathanu, 2020; Sattu et al., 2024),
while AI-enabled performance appraisal has received comparatively less focused attention.
This paper responds to that gap by synthesising the existing literature, drawing out its major
themes and proposing practical suggestions for Indian organisations. The paper is organised
as follows: Section 2 states the objectives; Section 3 reviews the literature; Section 4 explains
the methodology; Section 5 presents findings and discussion; Section 6 offers suggestions;
Section 7 notes limitations and future research directions; and Section 8 concludes.
2. OBJECTIVES OF THE STUDY
The study is guided by the following objectives:
 To underst and the conceptual shift from traditional to AI -enabled performance
appraisal.
 To review the existing literature on AI in HRM and performance appraisal, with
emphasis on Indian authors and the Indian context.
 To identify the key opportunities, challenges and concerns associated with using AI in
performance appraisal.
 To suggest a practical human -in-the-loop approach and recommendations for Indian
organisations, policymakers and researchers.
3. REVIEW OF LITERATURE
3.1 Performance appraisal: evolution and issues in the Indian context
Appraisal practice in India has moved from confidential, trait -based reports towards
objective-based and competency -based systems that use tools such as management by
objectives, balanced scorecards, 360 -degree feedback and, more  recently, continuous
performance management (Rao, 2004; Aswathappa, 2013; Dessler & Varkkey, 2015). Indian
HRD scholarship has long stressed the developmental purpose of appraisal, emphasising
feedback, counselling and employee growth alongside evaluation  (Rao, 2004). Reviewing a
century of research, DeNisi and Murphy (2017) argue that the field has moved beyond a
narrow concern with rating accuracy towards the wider performance management process,
including feedback and the social context in which apprais al takes place. Indian textbooks
and practitioner works nevertheless continue to document persistent problems of rater bias,
poor differentiation among employees and low employee trust in ratings (Bhattacharyya,
2011; Kandula, 2013).

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 226

3.2 AI in HRM: global and Indian scholarship
Budhwar et al. (2022), in a systematic review, describe research on AI -based HRM as
growing but fragmented and propose a conceptual framework and research propositions.
Kaushal et al. (2023) map the field through a systematic literat ure review and bibliometric
analysis and set out a future research agenda, while Basu et al. (2023) use a configurational
approach to explain both beneficial and reactionary outcomes when AI interacts with HRM.
Chowdhury et al. (2023) argue for an AI capab ility framework to unlock value in HRM, and
Prikshat et al. (2023) examine the antecedents, assimilation and multilevel consequences of
AI-augmented HRM. At the practice level, Malik et al. (2022) present a multinational
enterprise case in which AI -enabled HR supports cost -effectiveness and individualised
employee experiences.
Within the Indian setting, Sivathanu and Pillai (2018) discuss how Industry 4.0 technologies
are disrupting HR under the label “Smart HR 4.0”, and Rana and Sharma (2019) describe
emerging HR practices in Industry 4.0. Pillai and Sivathanu (2020) surveyed 562 HR and
talent acquisition managers using the Technology –Organisation–Environment (TOE) and
Task–Technology Fit (TTF) frameworks and found that task and technology characteristi cs
influence task –technology fit, which together with adoption influences actual usage of AI;
stickiness to traditional methods weakened the link between adoption and usage. Sattu et al.
(2024), studying 198 HR professionals in Indian IT companies, report that perceived value,
arising from perceived benefits and sacrifices, predicts the intention to adopt AI, and that HR
readiness moderates this relationship. Bhatt and Muduli (2023) review AI in learning and
development, an area closely connected to appraisal outcomes.
3.3 AI in performance appraisal and evaluation
Applications of AI to appraisal draw on the same building blocks as other people analytics:
machine-learning models that predict performance or flag anomalies, natural language
processing that analyses written feedback, and dashboards that consolidate work data. Tambe
et al. (2019), in an influential review of AI in HRM, note that HR outcomes such as
performance are complex, that data about them are often sparse and noisy, and that
algorithmic deci sions raise fairness, legal and employee -reaction challenges. Two
experimental studies provide more direct evidence on evaluation. Qin et al. (2023), in a field
experiment comparing AI with human managers in structured, data -intensive performance
evaluation, found that employees considered AI fairer and more accurate than the average
human manager, but also that human managers’ perceived fairness played a first -order role in
employee outcomes. Biswas et al. (2024), using a 2×2 between -subject experiment on
performance feedback, support the use of combined AI–human feedback to alleviate negative
employee perceptions.
3.4 Fairness, ethics and employee capability
Varma et al. (2023) assess AI in people management through an ethical lens, highlighting the
need to examine fairness, accountability and human dignity when algorithms shape decisions
about employees. Basu et al. (2023) draw attention to the threat of job loss that accompanies

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 227

AI adoption and the reactionary responses it can provoke. Jaiswal et al. (202 2) discuss the
upskilling of employees for AI in multinational corporations, which matters for appraisal
because employees’ capability and confidence shape acceptance of AI -based systems. Taken
together, this body of work suggests that technical accuracy a lone does not guarantee
acceptance; legitimacy depends on ethics, capability and communication.  The review reveals
three gaps. First, India -specific empirical work on AI in performance appraisal, as distinct
from recruitment, is limited. Second, evidence o n employee acceptance across sectors,
including manufacturing, public-sector organisations and MSMEs, is scarce. Third, there are
few practical frameworks that combine the efficiency of AI with explicit fairness and privacy
safeguards. This paper attempts to address the third gap conceptually and to point to the first
two for future empirical work.
4. RESEARCH METHODOLOGY
4.1 Research design
The study is descriptive and qualitative in nature and is based entirely on secondary data. It
follows a narrative review design supported by thematic content analysis. No primary data
were collected from respondents; the paper’s contribution is a structured synthesis of existing
knowledge and a conceptual framework derived from it.
4.2 Sources and search strategy
Literature was drawn from Google Scholar and from the platforms of major publishers of
peer-reviewed management and HRM research (Emerald, Elsevier, Springer, Taylor &
Francis and Sage), together with standard Indian and international textbooks on performance
management and government policy documents. Search strings combined terms such as
“artificial intelligence”, “machine learning”, “people analytics”, “performance appraisal”,
“performance management”, “performance evaluation”, “HRM” and “India”.
4.3 Selection criteria
Peer-reviewed journal articles, scholarly books and official policy documents were included
if they addressed AI in HRM, performance appraisal or performance management. Priority
was given to works by Indian authors and to studies set in Indian  organisations; a small
number of international experimental studies were retained for comparative evidence on how
employees respond to AI -based evaluation. Vendor marketing material, non -scholarly blogs
and articles unrelated to HRM were excluded. Recent work (approximately 2017 onwards)
was preferred, supplemented by earlier foundational texts on appraisal.
4.4 Method of analysis
The selected works were read closely and coded for the AI application discussed, the
appraisal stage addressed, reported benefits, reported concerns, and contextual factors. Codes
were grouped iteratively into themes, which are presented in Section 5. The stages of the
appraisal cycle (goal setting, monitoring, feedback, evaluation, calibration and development)
were used as an org anising lens to map AI applications, and Tables 2 and 3 are analytical
syntheses prepared by the author rather than reports of a single empirical study.

4.5 Methodological limitations
The review is purposive and selective rather than exhaustive, and the fi eld is evolving
quickly, so newer studies may refine or contradict the themes reported here. The findings are
therefore best read as well-grounded propositions to be tested empirically.
5. DISCUSSION AND FINDINGS
The thematic synthesis produced six themes, which are discussed in turn.
5.1 Theme 1: AI applications span the entire appraisal cycle
The literature indicates that AI is not confined to a single appraisal step. It can support goal
setting, ongoing monitoring, feedback, rating, calibration and deve lopment planning, as
summarised in Table 2. Each application brings a distinct benefit but also a distinct risk,
which is why the design of the system, and not simply the choice of tool, determines
outcomes.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 229

5.2 Theme 2: greater objectivity, but the risk of algorithmic bias
The most frequently cited promise of AI in appraisal is consistency. Algorithms apply the
same rules to every employee and are unaffected by mood, fatigue or personal liking. Qin et
al. (2023) found that employees regarded AI as both fairer and more accura te than the
average human manager when performance was evaluated on structured, data -intensive
criteria. This promise is, however, conditional. AI models learn from historical data, and if
past ratings, promotions or work assignments reflected bias, models  can reproduce that bias
at scale (Tambe et al., 2019). AI also tends to privilege what can be measured, so roles that
involve a large share of collaborative, creative or relational work may be undervalued. In
Indian organisations, where workforces are div erse in language, region, gender and
educational background, the possibility of unintended disadvantage calls for deliberate testing
of models rather than an assumption of neutrality.
5.3 Theme 3: movement from annual events to continuous feedback
AI makes it feasible to shift from a once -a-year event to ongoing performance conversations.
Systems can aggregate work data, prompt timely feedback, summarise inputs from several
sources and alert managers to goal slippage or declining engagement. This fits the
developmental orientation long emphasised in Indian HRD scholarship (Rao, 2004) and the
view that appraisal should be understood as part of a wider performance management process
(DeNisi & Murphy, 2017). Continuous data capture can, however, be experienced as
surveillance if employees do not know what is collected and why, which links directly to the
ethical concerns raised by Varma et al. (2023).
5.4 Theme 4: employee perceptions of fairness, transparency and trust
Evidence on employee reactions is mixed. Q in et al. (2023) report favourable fairness and
accuracy perceptions of AI-based evaluation, and Biswas et al. (2024) support combining AI
and human feedback to alleviate negative perceptions and thereby improve the return on AI
investment. On the other ha nd, Basu et al. (2023) document reactionary outcomes, including
concerns about job loss, in AI –HRM interaction, and Pillai and Sivathanu (2020) find that
stickiness to traditional methods dampens actual usage of AI. Taken together, these studies
suggest that acceptance depends less on the technology itself than on how it is introduced: the
transparency of criteria, the opportunity for employee voice, and the visible role of human
managers in the final decision.
5.5 Theme 5: ethical, privacy and legal concerns
Appraisal data is among the most sensitive information an employer holds. Ethical concerns
identified in the literature include the opacity of models, the difficulty of contesting an
algorithmic assessment, and the risk that consequential decisions are taken with little hu man
review (Varma et al., 2023; Tambe et al., 2019). In India, the Digital Personal Data
Protection Act, 2023 introduces obligations on organisations that process individuals’ digital
personal data, including in the employment context; HR and legal teams s hould therefore
jointly examine how the Act applies to appraisal analytics, including purpose, security
safeguards and grievance redressal (Government of India, 2023). This paper does not offer

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 230

legal advice, and organisations should seek qualified counsel on compliance. An emerging
practice deserving early attention is the use of generative AI tools to draft appraisal
comments and summaries. Such tools can save managers’ time, but they introduce risks of
factual inaccuracy, generic or biased language, and i nadvertent disclosure of confidential
employee information to external platforms. Clear usage policies and human verification of
all generated text are therefore essential.
5.6 Theme 6: organisational readiness and managerial capability
Adoption research i n India shows that readiness matters. Sattu et al. (2024) report that HR
readiness moderates the relationship between perceived value and intention to adopt AI, and
Pillai and Sivathanu (2020) show that task –technology fit is central to actual usage.
Chowdhury et al. (2023) similarly emphasise organisational AI capabilities, while Jaiswal et
al. (2022) and Bhatt and Muduli (2023) underline the importance of upskilling and learning.
For appraisal, this implies that managers must be able to interpret AI outpu ts, explain them to
employees and override them where context demands. Without such capability, AI risks
becoming either an unquestioned authority or an ignored dashboard.
5.7 Overall synthesis
The six themes point to a consistent conclusion: AI can improv e the consistency, frequency
and developmental value of appraisal, but its benefits are realised only when it is embedded
in a transparent, ethically governed and human -led process. The suggestions in Section 6
translate this conclusion into practice.
6. SUGGESTIONS
6.1 A human-in-the-loop appraisal framework

assists at each appraisal stage while accountability for decisions remains with people.

6.2 Recommendations
 Keep a human decision -maker accountable. AI should recommend, not decide.
Final ratings that affect pay, promotion or termination should be made by a named
manager who records the reasoning, consistent with the human -led approach
supported by Qin et al. (2023) and Biswas et al. (2024).
 Ensure transparency and explainability. Employees should be told what data are
used, how they influence the assessment and how they can question it. Plain -language
explanations build the trust on which acceptance depends (Varma et al., 2023).
 Audit for bias and validate models regularly. Organisations should test outcomes
across gender, age, location, language and job family, and retrain or withdraw models
that produce unjustified differences (Tambe et al., 2019).
 Pilot before  scaling and involve employees. A phased rollout with employee
representatives, feedback loops and clear success measures reduces resistance and
exposes problems early (Pillai & Sivathanu, 2020).
 Protect privacy and comply with law. Collect only data relev ant to performance,
secure them, define retention limits and align practice with the Digital Personal Data
Protection Act, 2023 after legal review (Government of India, 2023).
 Build AI literacy among managers and HR professionals. Training should cover
how to read AI outputs, recognise limitations, explain results to employees and
override them when justified (Jaiswal et al., 2022; Bhatt & Muduli, 2023; Sattu et al.,
2024).
 Pair AI with a culture of continuous feedback. Technology cannot substitute for
the quality of manager –employee conversations. Managers should be trained in
coaching and developmental feedback in line with the HRD tradition (Rao, 2004).

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 232

 Broaden the metrics. Include qualitative evidence, team contribution and behaviours
alongside quantifiable outputs so that AI does not reward only with what is easy to
count.
 Provide an appeal and grievance mechanism. Employees should be able to contest
an assessment and obtain a human review within a defined time.
 Govern generative AI use. Issue guidelines on the use of generative AI for drafting
appraisal content, prohibit entering confidential employee data into unapproved
external tools, and require human verification.
 Support policy and research. Industry bodies, academia and policymakers should
collaborate on sector -specific guidance and on empirical studies across IT/ITeS,
banking, manufacturing, public -sector organisations and MSMEs, consistent with
national AI policy discussions (NITI Aayog, 2018).
7. LIMITATIONS AND SCOPE FOR FUTURE RESEARCH
This pa per is conceptual and based on secondary sources; it does not test relationships
empirically. Future research could (i) survey employees and managers in Indian organisations
to measure perceived fairness, trust and acceptance of AI -based appraisal; (ii) co nduct
longitudinal or experimental studies comparing AI -assisted and conventional appraisal on
outcomes such as engagement, performance and turnover intention; (iii) compare sectors and
organisation sizes, including public -sector bodies and MSMEs; (iv) exa mine how gender,
language and regional diversity interact with algorithmic evaluation; and (v) study the
implications of generative AI for appraisal writing and feedback.
8. CONCLUSION
Artificial intelligence offers Indian organisations a real opportunity to make performance
appraisal more consistent, more continuous and more closely tied to employee development.
The literature reviewed shows that AI can extend across the whole appraisal cycle and that
employees may, in some circumstances, perceive AI-based evaluation as fair and accurate. At
the same time, the same literature warns of algorithmic bias, opaque models, privacy risks,
employee anxiety and uneven organisational readiness.  The central message of this paper is
that the value of AI in appraisal de pends less on the sophistication of the algorithm than on
the quality of the surrounding governance: transparent criteria, regular bias audits, data
protection, capable managers and a firm principle that people remain accountable for
decisions about people. Organisations that adopt AI as a partner to human judgement, and not
as a substitute for it, are more likely to gain both efficiency and legitimacy. As Indian
employers deepen their use of AI, empirical research on employee experience in the Indian
context will be essential to refine these propositions.

AI AND THE FUTURE OF COMMERCE AND BUSINESS
Mrs. D, Christinal Esther Rani
 Part-Time Ph.D. Research Scholar
 Department of Economics
 Presidency College, Chennai-05.
Dr. P. Jeevarathinam
 Associate Professor
 Department of Economics
Presidency College, Chennai-05.
ABSTRACT
Artificial Intelligence (AI) has moved from experimental technology to a core driver of value
creation across commerce and business. This paper examines the trajectory of AI adoption in
commerce and management and projects the directions in which the technology is likely to
reshape markets, organisations, and w ork over the coming decade. Building peer-reviewed
literature and recent industry research, the study traces how generative AI, predictive
analytics, and autonomous decision systems are transforming marketing, retail, supply chains,
finance, human resource management, and strategic decision -making. It highlights that a
substantial share of the economic value from AI is expected to be concentrated in customer
operations, marketing and sales, software engineering, and research and development, while
entire categories of routine work are likely to be automated within the next two to three
decades. At the same time, the paper considers the structural challenges that will determine
how this future unfolds: data privacy and governance, algorithmic bias, workforce
displacement and reskilling, high im plementation costs, and the need for explainable and
trustworthy AI. The paper concludes that the future of commerce and business will be
defined less by whether organisations adopt AI and more by how responsibly and
strategically they integrate it, arguin g that sustainable competitive advantage will accrue to
businesses that pair AI capability with ethical governance, human oversight, and continuous
workforce adaptation.
Keywords: Artificial Intelligence, Future of Commerce, Future of Business, Generative AI,
Digital Transformation, Automation
INTRODUCTION
Commerce and business have entered a period of structural transformation driven by rapid
advances in Artificial Intelligence (AI). What began as isolated applications of machine
learning in fraud detectio n or product recommendation has evolved into a general -purpose
technology capable of reasoning over unstructured data, generating content, and supporting
autonomous decision -making across nearly every business function. Industry analysis
suggests that generative AI alone could add between USD 2.6 trillion and USD 4.4 trillion in
annual value to the global economy, concentrated primarily in customer operations,

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 236

marketing and sales, software engineering, and research and development functions
(McKinsey Global Institute, 2023). This scale of projected impact has made the question of
AI's future role in commerce and management a central concern for scholars, executives, and
policymakers alike.
The future of commerce is increasingly being shaped by intelligent, a daptive, and self -
improving systems that personalise the customer journey, optimise pricing and inventory in
real time, and compress the time between demand signal and fulfilment (Berkman, 2022). In
parallel, the future of business management is being rede fined by AI's capacity to augment
strategic decision -making, automate human resource processes, and enable scenario -based
planning grounded in continuously updated data (Loureiro, Guerreiro, & Tussyadiah, 2021).
Yet this transition is not without friction.  Concerns about data privacy, algorithmic bias,
workforce displacement, and the opacity of AI -driven decisions have prompted calls for
stronger governance frameworks and more transparent, explainable AI systems. This paper
examines where AI in commerce and  business currently stands, synthesises recent scholarly
and industry perspectives on where it is heading, and considers the opportunities and risks
that will shape that future.
REVIEW OF LITERATURE
Loureiro, Guerreiro, and Tussyadiah (2021) analysed 404 a rticles on AI in business drawn
from the Web of Science and Scopus databases and used a text -mining approach based on
Latent Dirichlet Allocation to map the evolution of AI research in the business context. Their
study traces the growth trajectory of the f ield, identifies the most influential publication
venues, and sets out a research agenda spanning AI's organisational, ethical, and strategic
dimensions, providing an empirical foundation for understanding how the discipline has
matured and where future research is headed.
Huang and Rust (2021) proposed a strategic framework describing how AI progresses
through mechanical, thinking, and feeling stages as it is adopted within marketing functions.
Their framework argues that mechanical AI is best suited to st andardisation and efficiency,
thinking AI to data -driven personalisation, and feeling AI to relationship -building tasks that
require emotional and social intelligence, offering a roadmap for how marketing
organisations should sequence AI investment as the technology matures.
Brynjolfsson, Li, and Raymond (2023), in a large -scale study of customer support agents
using a generative AI assistant, found that access to the tool increased worker productivity by
an average of 14 percent, with the largest gains amo ng novice and lower -skilled workers.
Their findings suggest that generative AI can function as a form of on -the-job learning that
narrows performance gaps between new and experienced employees, reshaping how
organisations think about training and workforce development.
Grewal, Satornino, Davenport, and Guha (2025) examined how generative AI is reshaping
the practice and theory of marketing, arguing that the technology is shifting marketing from a
primarily human -led, campaign -based activity toward a continu ous, AI -assisted process of

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 237

content generation, personalisation, and real-time customer engagement. The study highlights
emerging risks, including diminished brand distinctiveness and consumer scepticism toward
AI-generated content, alongside the clear productivity gains the technology enables.
Panditharathna, Liu, Nasir, D'Elia, Rai, and Mujawar (2024) conducted a systematic review
of AI in e -commerce using the PRISMA framework and the Scopus database, identifying
seven thematic clusters spanning advanced computational techniques, AI -driven
personalisation, supply chain optimisation, and data -driven retail systems. The review calls
for future research on ethical AI, consumer trust, and the integration of AI with emerging
technologies such as blockchain and quantum computing in e-commerce contexts.
The McKinsey Global Institute (2023) assessed the economic potential of generative AI
across 63 use cases in 16 business functions, projecting that the technology could automate
activities currently occupying 60 to 70 percent of employees' time and could raise the impact
of AI overall by 15 to 40 percent when embedded into existing enterprise software. The
report frames workforce transition and reskilling as central preconditions for capturing this
economic potential responsibly.
OBJECTIVES OF THE STUDY
● To examine the current trajectory of AI adoption across key commerce and business
management functions.
● To synthesise scholarly and industry research on the likely future direction of AI in
commerce and business over the next decade.
● To identify the structural challenges and ethical considerations that will shape a
responsible AI-driven future for commerce and management.
AI AND THE FUTURE OF COMMERCE
Hyper-Personalisation and Customer Experience
The future of commerce is moving decisively toward hyper -personalisation, where AI
systems anticipate customer needs before they are explicitly expressed. Building on today's
recommendation engines, next -generation systems will combine behavioural data, location,
mood signals, and real -time context to curate entire shopping journeys rather than individual
products (Grewal, Satornino, Davenport, & Guha, 2025). Retailers are expecte d to deploy
generative AI to create dynamic product descriptions, personalised advertising creative, and
conversational shopping assistants that operate across voice, chat, and augmented -reality
interfaces.
Autonomous and Predictive Supply Chains
Supply chains of the future are expected to become increasingly self -correcting, using
predictive analytics and machine learning to anticipate disruptions, rebalance inventory, and
reroute logistics automatically. Early adopters of AI -enabled supply chain management have

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 238

already reported meaningful reductions in logistics costs and improvements in service levels,
and this trajectory is expected to accelerate as AI is combined with Internet of Things sensors
and blockchain -based traceability to create supply  chains that are simultaneously more
efficient, transparent, and resilient (Panditharathna et al., 2024).
The Expansion of Quick Commerce
Quick commerce (Q -commerce) is projected to expand further as AI improves demand
forecasting, dark -store inventory pla cement, and last -mile delivery routing. AI's ability to
anticipate hyperlocal demand patterns allow businesses to stock the right products in the right
micro-fulfilment centres ahead of demand, compressing delivery windows while reducing
waste, a dynamic expected to intensify competition around speed and convenience in retail.
Generative AI in Marketing and Advertising
Marketing is likely to be among the functions most transformed by generative AI, with
McKinsey estimating that marketing and sales together account for a substantial share of
generative AI's total projected economic value (McKinsey Global Institute, 2023). Future
marketing organisations are expected to rely on AI not only for targeting and segmentation
but for generating creative content itsel f, while simultaneously navigating new risks around
content authenticity, brand differentiation, and consumer trust (Grewal, Satornino,
Davenport, & Guha, 2025).
AI AND THE FUTURE OF BUSINESS AND MANAGEMENT
Strategic Decision-Making
Future management pract ice is expected to be increasingly data -native, with AI systems
continuously simulating scenarios, stress -testing strategic options, and surfacing competitive
intelligence in real time. Rather than replacing managerial judgement, AI is expected to
function as a decision -support layer that widens the evidence base available to executives,
enabling faster and more confident responses to market shifts (Loureiro, Guerreiro, &
Tussyadiah, 2021).
The Future Workforce and Human Resource Management
Human resource management is projected to move toward continuous, AI -assisted workforce
planning: predictive attrition models, adaptive learning platforms, and AI -mediated
recruitment are expected to become standard rather than experimental. Evidence already
suggests that  generative AI tools narrow the performance gap between novice and
experienced workers by acting as an always -available coach, a dynamic expected to reshape
corporate training investment over the next decade (Brynjolfsson, Li, & Raymond, 2023).
Financial Services and Risk Management
In finance, the future points toward increasingly autonomous fraud detection, real -time credit
underwriting, and robo -advisory platforms capable of continuously rebalancing portfolios

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 239

against a client's evolving goals and risk t olerance. As these systems scale, regulatory
scrutiny is expected to intensify, pushing financial institutions toward more explainable and
auditable AI models.
Operations, Automation, and Project Management
Operations and project management are expected to see the deepest levels of task automation,
with AI systems handling scheduling, predictive maintenance, and resource allocation with
minimal human intervention. This is projected to free managers to focus on innovation and
cross-functional coordination ra ther than routine monitoring, echoing McKinsey's estimate
that a large share of current work activities is technically automatable with existing and
emerging AI capabilities (McKinsey Global Institute, 2023).
ECONOMIC AND WORKFORCE OUTLOOK

business will continue to expand rapidly. Table 1 summarises selected projections that
illustrate the scale of the expected transformation.

These figures suggest that the future competitive landscape in commerce and business will

treat it as a peripheral tool. However, capturing this value at scale is expected to depend on
parallel investment in workforce reskilling, since a large proportion of current job tasks are
projected to be automatable well before equivalent numbe rs of new AI -augmented roles are
created (McKinsey Global Institute, 2023).

CHALLENGES ON THE PATH TO AN AI-DRIVEN FUTURE
● High implementation costs: Building future -ready AI capability requires sustained
investment in infrastructure, legacy -system modernisation, and specialised talent,
which can be prohibitive for small and mid-sized firms.
● Data privacy and governance: As AI systems become more autonomous, the volume
and sensitivity of data they process increases, heightening risks around privacy,
security, and regulatory compliance if governance frameworks lag behind adoption.
● Algorithmic bias and fairness: AI systems trained on historical data risk reproducing
and amplifying existing biases, making ethics -by-design and responsible AI
governance central to sustainable future adoption.
● Workforce displacement and reskilling: While AI is expected to create new categories
of work, it is also projected to automate a significant share of existing tasks, making
large-scale reskilling and changing management a precondition for an equitable
transition.
● Integration with legacy systems: Embedding AI into established enterprise workflows,
CRM platforms, and organisational structures often generates technical and cultural
friction that slows the realisation of projected benefits.
● Explainability and trust: As AI takes on a greater share of decision -making in
commerce and management, the opacity of many models poses a growing challenge
to accountability, regulatory compliance, and stakeholder trust.
FUTURE OUTLOOK
Looking ahead, the future of commerce and business is likely to be defined by the
convergence of AI with other emerging technologies. The integration of AI with the Internet
of Things and blockchain is expected to produce supply chains that are simultaneousl y more
transparent, efficient, and resilient (Panditharathna et al., 2024). The development of
explainable AI models is expected to progressively address concerns around trust and
transparency, making AI -driven decisions more interpretable to regulators, m anagers, and
consumers alike. Marketing is likely to continue its shift toward continuous, AI -generated,
and hyper -personalised engagement, even as businesses work to preserve brand
distinctiveness and consumer trust in an increasingly AI -saturated content  environment
(Grewal, Satornino, Davenport, & Guha, 2025). In management, strategic decision -making is
expected to become progressively more scenario-driven and evidence-based, supported by AI
systems capable of simulating multiple future states in real ti me (Loureiro, Guerreiro, &
Tussyadiah, 2021). Perhaps most significantly, the future workforce is likely to be
characterised by close human -AI collaboration rather than wholesale replacement, with new
professional categories emerging around AI governance, prompt and data engineering, and
AI-augmented strategic planning, even as reskilling becomes a continuous organisational
responsibility rather than a one-time initiative (McKinsey Global Institute, 2023).

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management

ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                 DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 241

CONCLUSION
Artificial Intelligence is set to remain  the single most influential force reshaping commerce
and business over the coming decade. The evidence reviewed in this paper indicates that the
future will bring deeper personalisation in customer experience, more autonomous and
predictive supply chains,  increasingly data -driven strategic management, and a workforce
that works alongside, rather than in place of, intelligent systems. At the same time, the scale
of projected economic value, running into trillions of dollars annually, will only be captured
sustainably if organisations address the parallel challenges of data governance, algorithmic
fairness, workforce reskilling, and explainability. The future of commerce and business will
therefore not be determined purely by the pace of technological advance ment, but by how
effectively organisations, regulators, and workers adapt together to build an AI -enabled
economy that is productive, trustworthy, and inclusive.

A Study on Customer Perception of Artificial Intelligence in Banking Fraud
Detection
P. SATHYA
 Assistant Professor
Department of corporate Secretaryship
, Chennai national Arts and Science College, avadi-600054,

ABSTRACT:
Artificial Intelligence (AI) is  increasingly being used in the banking sector to improve
security and detect fraudulent activities. This study focuses on customers’ awareness, trust,
and perception of Artificial Intelligence in banking fraud detection. The study examines
whether custome rs’ awareness of AI is related to their perception of its effectiveness in
detecting fraud, whether trust in AI influences their feeling of safety, and whether the
perceived usefulness of AI is related to their belief that it can reduce financial losses ca used
by fraud. The study is  based on primary data collected from bank customers through a
structured questionnaire. The collected data were analyzed using the Chi -Square Test to
determine the relationship between the selected variables. The findings show t hat there is a
significant relationship between AI awareness and perceived effectiveness trust in AI and
perceived safety, and perceived usefulness of AI and reduction of financial losses. The study
concludes that increasing customer awareness, trust, and understanding of AI can support the
effective adoption of AI-based fraud detection systems in banking.

INTRODUCTION:
Nowadays, banking has become more digital and convenient, but at the same time, the risk of
fraud has also increased. Banks deal with a hu ge number of transactions every day, making it
difficult to identify every suspicious activity using traditional methods. This is where
Artificial Intelligence (AI) can play an important role.  AI can help banks detect fraud by
studying customers’ transaction patterns and identifying activities that seem unusual or
suspicious. For example, if a customer suddenly makes a large transaction from an unfamiliar
location or device, AI can detect this change and alert the bank. Machine learning can also
learn from previous fraud cases and become better at recognizing similar patterns in the
future. Using AI for fraud detection can help banks respond more quickly, reduce financial
losses, and make banking safer for customers. As digital banking continues to grow, AI is
becoming an important  tool for protecting both banks and their customers from fraudulent
activities. Banking has changed a lot with the development of digital technology. Today,
people can transfer money, pay bills, shop online, and manage thei r accounts without visiting
a bank.  While these services make banking easier and faster, they have also increased the
chances of fraud, such as unauthorized transactions, identity theft, phishing, and account
hacking.
Detecting these fraudulent activities can be difficult because banks process millions of
transactions every day. Traditional fraud detection methods mainly depend on fixed rules and
manual checks, which may not always be able to identify new or complicated types of

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 243

fraud. Artificial Intelligen ce (AI) can help overcome these limitations by analysing large
amounts of data quickly.
AI systems can study a customer's normal banking behaviour and identify unusual changes.
For example, if a customer normally makes small transactions in their local area but suddenly
makes a very large transaction from a different location, the AI system can identify it as
suspicious. It can then alert the bank or  ask the customer to verify the transaction.  Machine
learning, which is a part of AI, can learn from previ ous fraud cases and recognize patterns
that may indicate fraudulent activity. AI can also monitor transactions in real time, helping
banks respond to suspicious activities before significant financial damage occurs.  Another
important benefit of AI is that it can reduce the workload of bank employees. Instead  of
manually checking every transaction, employees can focus on transactions that AI has
identified as high -risk. This can make the fraud investigation process faster and more
efficient.
AI can also help  detect credit card fraud, online payment fraud, identity theft, account
takeovers, and suspicious money transfers . By improving the speed and accuracy of fraud
detection, AI can help protect customers' money and personal information.  However, AI is
not completely risk-free. Banks need to protect customer data, maintain privacy, and regularly
update their AI systems because fraudsters are constantly developing new methods. Human
supervision is still important to make sure that genuine transactions are not  incorrectly
blocked. Overall, AI has become an  important technology  in modern  banking. When  used
properly along with human expertise and strong security measures, it can help banks detect
fraud more quickly, reduce financial losses, and provide customers with a safer and more
secure banking experience.
Review of Literature: Use of AI in Banking to Detect Fraud:
Artificial Intelligence (AI) has become an important technology in the banking sector,
particularly for identifying and preventing financial fraud.  Recent studies show that machine
learning, deep learning, graph -based techniques, and explainable AI can help banks identify
suspicious transactions more quickly and accurately than traditional rule-based systems.
1. Ukirde, Gaikwad, Shinde and Hingmire (202 4): Examined an AI -based fraud detection
system for financial risk management. Their work focused on using machine -learning
techniques and pattern recognition to identify fraudulent activities, including online
transaction fraud and fraudulent loan applications. The study supports the use of AI as a tool
for improving risk management in financial institutions.

2. Hernandez Aros et al. (2024) conducted a  literature review on  financial fraud detection
using machine learning techniques. Their study highlighted t he growing use of machine
learning to identify fraudulent patterns and improve the ability of financial institutions  to
detect fraud in large and complex datasets

3. Goyal, Garg and Malik (2025): Examined the adoption of AI for credit-risk assessment
and fraud detection in banking services . Their study considered the factors that influence

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 244

the continued use of  AI in banking and highlighted the importance of technological
knowledge in successfully adopting  AI-based systems. This shows that having AI
technology alone is not enough; banks also need appropriate knowledge and infrastructure to
use it effectively.

4. Rahim et al. (2026): Studied AI-driven financial fraud detection in Pakistan's banking
sector. Their research focused not only on the potential of AI but  also on the challenges
involved in putting AI systems into actual banking operations. The study highlights the
importance of moving from strategic plans to practical implementation when adopting AI for
fraud detection.

5. Suganthi et al. (2026): Proposed DEFENSE-BANK, a deep -learning framework designed
for fraud detection in the ban king sector. The research responds to the increasing risks
associated with online banking and sophisticated cyberattacks. It demonstrates the growing
use of advanced deep -learning approaches to improve fraud detection in banking
environments.
OBJECTIVES:
1. To examine the level of awareness and perception of customers regarding the use of
AI in banking for fraud detection.
2. To assess the effectiveness and usefulness of AI in detecting and preventing banking
fraud, particularly in identifying suspicious transactions and reducing financial losses.
3. To evaluate customers’ trust, concerns, and willingness to accept AI-
based fraud detection systems in banking.
METHODOLOGY:
This study is conducted to understand customers’ awareness, trust, and opinions about the
use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) in detecting fraud in the banking sector. For this study,
primary data was collected from 50 respondents using a structured questionnaire. The
respondents were selected through convenience sampling based on their availability and
willingness to participate. The questionnaire included questions related to awareness of  AI,
trust in AI, feeling of safety, usefulness of AI, and its effectiveness in de tecting fraudulent
transactions. The collected responses were analysed using simple percentage analysis and
the Chi-Square Test. The Chi-Square Test was used to find out whether there is a significant
relationship between  the selected variables. The study was carried out at a 5% level of
significance to understand how customers view the role of AI in  improving fraud detection
and reducing financial losses in banking.

THEORETICAL BACKGROUND:
1. Artificial Intelligence in Banking
Artificial intelligence (AI) refers to the use of computer systems and technologies that can

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 245

perform activities that normally require human intelligence. These activities include learning
from information, recognising patterns, analysing data, making predi ctions, and supporting
decision-making. In recent years, AI has become an important part of the banking industry
because banks handle a very large amount of financial and customer data every day.
Banks use AI in several areas, including customer service, c redit assessment, personalised
banking services, transaction monitoring, and fraud detection. AI allows banks to process
large amounts of information quickly and identify patterns that may not be easily noticed
through traditional methods. This makes AI pa rticularly useful for improving banking
security.
2. Artificial Intelligence and Fraud Detection
Banking fraud refers to illegal activities carried out to obtain money, financial information,
or other benefits through deception or unauthorised transactions. E xamples include credit
card fraud, online banking fraud, identity theft, phishing, account takeover, and unauthorised
digital transactions.
Traditional fraud detection systems generally work using predefined rules. For example, a
bank may flag a transaction when it exceeds a particular amount or occurs in an unusual
location. Although these methods are useful, they may not always identify new or
complicated forms of fraud.
AI-based fraud detection provides a more advanced approach. AI systems can examine
transaction history and identify unusual patterns in customer behaviour. Machine learning
algorithms can learn from previous transaction data and identify transactions that appear
different from normal customer activity. When suspicious behaviour is detected, the bank
can investigate the transaction or take preventive action.
Therefore, AI can help banks detect fraud more quickly and improve the  overall security of
digital banking services.
3. Machine Learning in Fraud Detection
Machine learning is one of the important technologies used in AI -based fraud detection. It
allows computer systems to learn from existing data and improve their ability to i dentify
patterns without requiring every situation to be manually programmed.
For example, if a customer's normal transactions usually occur in a particular location and
within a certain spending range, a sudden transaction involving unusual behaviour may be
identified as suspicious. The system can compare the new transaction with previous patterns
and assign a risk level.
Machine learning can therefore help banks identify suspicious transactions, reduce the time
required for fraud investigation, and  support faster responses to possible fraudulent
activities.
4. Detection of Suspicious Transactions
A suspicious transaction is a transaction that differs significantly from a customer's normal

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 246

financial behaviour or appears to contain characteristics associated with fraudulent activity.
AI systems can analyse several factors, such as transaction amount, location, time,
frequency, device information, and previous transaction behaviour.  Instead of examining
every transaction manually, AI can automatically analyse large numbers  of transactions and
identify those that require further attention. This can help banks focus their resources on
potentially risky transactions. The ability to identify suspicious transactions a t an early stage
is important because early detection can reduce the potential damage caused by fraud.
5. Customer Awareness of AI
Customer awareness refers to the extent to which customers understand AI and its use in
banking services. Customers who are aware of AI -based fraud detection may have a better
understanding of how banks monitor transactions and protect their accounts. Awareness can
influence customers' perceptions of new technologies. If customers understand the purpose
and benefits of AI, they  may be more comfortable using AI -supported banking services. On
the other hand, a lack of awareness may create confusion or  uncertainty about how AI
operates.
6. Customer Trust in AI
Trust is an important factor in the adoption of technology in banking. Customers provide
banks with sensitive financial and personal information, so they need to have confidence that
the technology used by banks is reliable and secure.  Trust in AI means that customers
believe AI systems can perform their functions accurately,  securely, and fairly. If customers
trust AI, they may be more willing to accept its use for monitoring transactions and
preventing fraud. However, concerns about privacy, incorrect decisions, data security, and
lack of human involvement can affect customer trust. Therefore, banks need to communicate
clearly about how AI is being used and how customer information is protected.
7. Perceived Usefulness of AI
Perceived usefulness refers to the extent to which a person believes that using a particular
technology w ill provide benefits or improve performance. In the context of banking,
customers may consider AI useful if they believe that it can detect fraudulent transactions
quickly, improve account security, and reduce financial losses.
HYPOTHESIS
Hypothesis 1
H₀ (Null Hypothesis): There is no significant relationship between customers’ awareness of
AI in banking and their perception of the effectiveness of AI in detecting fraud.

Hypothesis 2
