AI and their willingness to feel safer when banks use AI for fraud detection.
H₁ (Alternative Hypothesis): There is a significant relationship between customers’ trust in
AI and their willingness to feel safer when banks use AI for fraud detection.
Hypothesis 3
H₀ (Null Hypothesis): There is no significant relationship between the perceived usefulness
of AI in detecting suspicious transactions and customers’ belief that AI can reduce
financial losses caused by fraud.
H₁  (Alternative  Hypothesis):  There  is  a significant  relationship between  the
perceived usefulness  of AI in  detecting suspicious transactions  and customers’ belief
that AI can reduce financial losses caused by fraud.
These hypotheses are directly  connected to your questionnaire questions, which will
make it easier to analyse the data using Chi-square, correlation, or other statistical tests

interpretation: Customers who are more aware of AI are more likely to believe that AI can
effectively detect banking fraud.
Hypothesis 2
Variables: Trust in AI × Feeling safer when banks use AI for fraud detection
Chi-Square result:
χ² = 34.662
p-value = 0.00442
Since p < 0.05, the null hypothesis is rejected.
interpretation: Customers who trust AI more are generally more likely to feel safer when AI
is used for fraud detection.
Hypothesis 3
Variables: Perceived usefulness of AI × Belief that AI can reduce financial losses
Chi-Square result:
χ²=34.573
p-value = 0.00455
Since p < 0.05, the null hypothesis is rejected.
interpretation: Customers who consider AI more useful are more likely to believe that it can
reduce financial losses from fraud.
FINDINGS
1. The study found that customers’ awareness of AI in banking has a significant relationship
with their perception of AI’s effectiveness in detecting fraud.
2. The study found that customers’ trust in AI is significantly related to their feeling of

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 249

safety when banks use AI for fraud detection.
3. The study found that customers who perceive AI as useful for detecting suspicious
transactions are more likely to believe that AI can reduce financial losses caused  by
fraud.
4. The Chi-Square analysis showed that all three hypotheses were statistically  significant at
the 5% level.
5. Overall, the findings indicate that awareness, trust, and perceived usefulness are
important factors influencing customers’ acceptance and positive perception of  AI-
based fraud detection.

CONCLUSION:
The study concludes that Artificial Intelligence plays an important role in improving fraud
detection in the banking sector. The analysis shows that customers’ awareness of AI is
significantly related to their perception of its effectiveness in detecting fraud. Similarly,
customers who have greater trust in  AI are more likely to feel safe when banks use  AI for
fraud detection. The study also concludes that customers who consider AI useful for
identifying suspicious transactions are more likely to believe that AI can reduce financial
losses caused by fraud. Since all three hypotheses were found to be statistically significant,
the study highlights the importance of customer awareness, trust, and perceived usefulness in
the successful adoption of AI-based fraud detection systems.

A STUDY ON THE EFFECTIVE MANAGEMENT OF REMOTE WORK FORCE
AT E-COMMERCE AND CONSULTING COMPANIES IN CHENNAI.
Dr. A. VIJAYALAKSHMI & Ms. DIVYADHARSHINI D
Department of Business Administration
Valliammal College for Women, Chennai
ABSTRACT

The goal of this study is to explore the key factors that contribute to the effective
management of a remote workforce at Ecommerce and Consulting Companies in Chennai.
The modern world is transforming under the influence of global changes. In the managements
of people's work, organisation, planning, control over implementation,  and motivation for
work results in the new conditions will change. The  management process in organisations
acquires specific characteristics. Today, such concepts as “office work”, “remote work”, and
“hybrid work” are used to describe a mode of work that combines traditional methods and
criteria of management activities with the latest ones that are becoming increasingly
important in management.
Key Words: Remote work, Hybrid Work, Ecommerce
INTRODUCTION
This study focuses on the effective  management of remote workforces, examining the key
strategies and best practices that enable organizations to successfully  lead virtual teams. As
businesses transition to a more flexible work environment, it is essential for managers and
leaders to adopt new approaches to overseei ng remote employees. Managing remote teams
requires a comprehensive understanding of how technology, leadership styles,
communication strategies, and organizational culture all play integral roles in fostering a
productive and engaged workforce. Unlike tra ditional office settings, where face -to-face
interaction and real-time feedback are the norm, remote work presents unique challenges that
must be navigated with careful consideration and planning.  While remote work offers
flexibility, it also brings unique challenges such as communication barriers, lack of face -to-
face interaction, and difficulty in fostering  a sense of belonging among employees. Therefore,
it is crucial to develop and implement effective strategies that not only manage remote work
but also enhance employee performance, engagement, and collaboration.
OBJECTIVES OF THE STUDY
 To study the effective management of remote work force at Ecommerce and
Consulting companies.
 To find the significant differences in employee well-being and job satisfaction in
remote settings across different age groups.
 To identity the relationship between the hybrid or remote work model that helps to
maintain a good work -life balance and communication within team is efficient,
regardless of whether working remotely or in the office
NEED FOR THE STUDY
 To understand the impact of remote work, it is essential to analyze how

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 251

organizations adapt to technological advancements and shifting work preferences.
 To determine whether remote work enhances productivity or creates challenges, it
is necessary to study factors such as motivation, isolation, and communication
gaps.
REVIEW OF LITERATURE
Volodymyr et al (2023) study aims to determine the level of manageme nt efficiency and the
problems of it providing in remote work mode. The efficiency level of the primary
management functions of planning, organisation, motivation and control in remote work was
defined by surveying employees of educational institutions, IT  companies and trade
enterprises.
Raj et al (2023) recommends that businesses become more adaptable as a result of
management stressing the value of work -life balance, assuring employee interest, and giving
accessible technologies that will boost business  performance. Understanding the interactions
amongst management and the impact on the organization requires an understanding of the
linkages between the various remote working indicators.
However, excessive reliance on digital communication can lead to "Zo om fatigue" and
misinterpretations, necessitating structured communication protocols (Wang et al., 2021).
Additionally, remote work can blur work -life boundaries, leading to burnout; therefore,
organizations must promote flexible work schedules and mental health support programs
(Kossek et al., 2021).
Conversely, other studies suggest that lack of supervision can lead to reduced accountability
and performance variability (Choudhury et al., 2020).
RESEARCH METHODOLOY

This study adopts descriptive research design to systematically observe, describe, and analyse
strategies for communication, productivity, and engagement  in remote teams without
manipulating variables. Data is collected from both primary and secondary sources, including
surveys and questionnaires. A combination of random sampling for surveys and
purposive sampling for interviews ensures representativeness.
The data analysis process involves thematic analysis for qualitative insights and descriptive
and inferential statistics for quantitative tre nds, using SPSS 30 Vision and Excel. This
methodology ensures a comprehensive understanding of effective remote workforce
management strategies.
This methodology adopted in this study is convenience sampling. The  sample size is 167
respondents. In this  research the sample were collected from the employees of Creo
Ecommerce and Consulting Private Limited.
The primary data was  collected in the form of questionnaire and the s econdary data are
gathered from surveys, studies or experiments that have been done by other for other research
The questionnaire for collection of data consists both structured (multiple -choice) and

Commerce and Management

unstructured (open-ended) questions for collection of responses.
The data collected was analyzed using appropriate tools relevant to this research
are:
1. Analysis of variance (ANOVA)
2. Correlation

FINDINGS AND IMPLICATIONS
The analysis revealed that age and professional  experience do not significantly influence
employees' perceptions of organizational support or their ability to achieve personal and career
goals in a remote setting. This indicates a generally uniform experience across demographics
when it comes to remote work satisfaction. There is no statistically significant difference
between the gro ups in terms of how they perceive  the organization's support for mental and
emotional well-being during remote work.  The absence of statistically significant differences
across age groups and     experience levels further  reinforces the importance of universal
strategies—rather than segmented approaches— in managing remote teams. Additionally, the
supportive role of organizational policies, especially  those fostering mental well-being and
flexible work conditions, emerged as essential factors in ensuri ng employee engagement and
satisfaction. Overall, the research underscores that remote work, when managed effectively
with the right communication tools, flexible policies, and a focus on well -being, can not only
sustain but enhance organizational performa nce and employee morale. These findings serve
as a guiding framework for HR leaders to develop inclusive, resilient, and future-ready remote
work cultures.
SUGGESTION
 Implement structured communication channels to support collaboration and reduce
miscommunication.
 Promote flexible work hours to help employees balance personal and
professional responsibilities.
 Provide mental health and wellness support through counselling services and regular
check-ins.
 Set clear expectations and deliverables to maintain alignment and accountability.
 Encourage virtual social interaction and team bonding to reduce feelings of isolation.

describe a mode of work that combines traditional methods and criteria of management
activities with the latest ones that are becoming increasingly important in management.  The
study on the effective management of the remote workforce has provided meaningful insights
into how organizations can adapt to evolving work environments while ensuring productivity,
employee satisfaction, and work-life balance.

ARTIFICIAL  INTELLIGENCE AND THE FUTURE OF IT PROFESSIONALS

Ms.MENAKA DEVI.G
Assistant Professor, Department of Business Administration
Valliammal College for Women, Chennai
Ms.VARNIKHA.D.V
II MBA, Department of Business Administration
Valliammal College for Women, Chennai
ABSTRACT
Artificial Intelligence (AI), particularly generative and agentic AI, is fundamentally reshaping
the Information Technology (IT) workforce. This conceptual study examines the dual nature
of AI’s impact —task automation and augmentation —on IT roles, skills, and organizational
structures. Drawing on recent industry analyses and theoretical perspectives on human -
machine collaboration, the paper proposes a conceptual framework centered on the shift from
execution-oriented to orchestration - and judgment -oriented work. Key findings highlight
productivity gains, the emergence of hybrid roles (e.g., AI integrators, agent orchestrators, AI
governance specialists), elevated demand for AI fluency combined with domain expertise and
soft skills, and risks concentrated amo ng routine and entry -level tasks. The study concludes
that proactive upskilling, ethical governance, and redesigned talent strategies are essential for
IT employees and organizations to thrive in an AI -augmented future. Implications are
discussed for practitioners, educators, and policymakers.
Keywords: Artificial Intelligence, IT Workforce, Job Transformation, Skills Evolution,
Human-AI Collaboration, Agentic AI, Upskilling and Reskilling, Redesign, Orchestration
and Judgment, AI Governance, Entry-Level Employment Risks
1.INTRODUCTION
The rapid advancement of Artificial Intelligence —encompassing machine learning,
generative AI, large language models (LLMs), and increasingly autonomous AI agents —
represents one of the most significant technological shifts fo r the IT sector. Unlike previous
waves of automation that primarily affected manufacturing or routine administrative work, AI
directly intersects with core IT activities: coding, system administration, data analysis,
cybersecurity monitoring, and support.  IT employees stand at the epicenter of this
transformation. They both develop AI systems and experience their effects on daily work.
Reports from Deloitte, PwC, the World Economic Forum (WEF), and others indicate that AI
is not simply displacing jobs whole sale but reengineering how technology teams are
structured, how work is performed, and which competencies hold value. Nearly 70% of tech
leaders plan to grow teams in response to generative AI through augmentation and
specialization rather than pure reduction. Productivity in highly AI-exposed organizations has
shown marked gains (e.g., up to 40% higher in some analyses), while skills requirements in
AI-exposed roles are evolving more than twice as fast as in less -exposed ones.  This
conceptual study addresses a gap in purely empirical or predictive literature by developing an
integrative framework for understanding AI’s impact on IT employees. It explores:

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 257

 How AI transforms existing IT roles (automation vs. augmentation).
 Emerging roles and skill demands.
 Organizational and individual adaptation pathways.
 Challenges, ethical considerations, and strategic recommendations.
2. OBJECTIVES
The primary aim of this conceptual study is to examine and conceptualize the transformative
impact of Artificial Intelligence—particularly generative and agentic AI—on the roles, skills,
and future trajectory of IT employees. Building on this overarching aim, the specific
objectives of the study are as follows:
2.1 To analyze the dual nature of AI’s impact
 The IT workforce by distinguishing between task automation (substitution of routine and
codifiable activities) and capability augmentation (enhancement of human performance
through collaboration with AI systems).
2.2 To examine the transformation of existing IT roles
It particular focus on software development, data -related functions, IT support and
operations, cybersecurity, and IT management, identifying which tasks are being automated,
redefined, or elevated.
2.3 To identify emerging and hybrid roles
It created or expanded by AI adoption, such as AI integrators, agent orchestrators, AI
governance specialists, human –AI collaboration designers, and related positions that bridge
technical and strategic responsibilities.
2.4 To explore the evolving skill requirements
 IT professionals in the AI era, emphasizing the growing importance of AI fluency combined
with domain expertise, critical thinking, systems thinking, ethical judgment, and interpersonal
competencies.
2.5 To assess the key challenges and risks
The associated with AI integration in IT, including potential displacement pressures on
routine and entry -level roles, skills gaps, ethical concerns, and organizational adaptation
barriers.
2.6 To propose strategic recommendations
 The IT employees, organiz ations, educational institutions, and policymakers to facilitate
effective upskilling, responsible AI adoption, and sustainable workforce transitions in an AI -
augmented future.
3. REVIEW OF LITERATURE
 Existing scholarship and industry analyses consistentl y portray Artificial Intelligence as a
dual force of disruption and augmentation within the IT workforce. Systematic reviews of IT
roles in the AI era highlight that software development, data -related positions, and IT
management are among the most affecte d, with AI delivering clear productivity gains
through automation of repetitive coding, monitoring, and support tasks while simultaneously
raising technical skill barriers and redefining responsibilities toward oversight, integration,
and ethical governance. Empirical reports from sources such as the World Economic Forum’s
Future of Jobs analyses, PwC’s AI Jobs Barometers, and Deloitte’s technology talent studies
further document net job creation potential alongside displacement risks, particularly for
routine and entry-level tasks, and emphasize the rapid evolution of required competencies —

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 258

AI fluency, agent orchestration, systems thinking, and human -centric skills such as judgment
and collaboration —changing more than twice as fast in highly AI -exposed occup ations.
Complementing these findings, conceptual and empirical works on human –machine
collaboration stress that the future of IT employment hinges less on outright substitution and
more on synergistic models in which humans provide creativity, contextual u nderstanding,
and ethical oversight while AI agents handle scale and pattern recognition. Studies and
projections (including U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics forecasts and Cisco -led workforce
consortia) identify emerging roles —AI architects, integrators, go vernance specialists, and
agent orchestrators—as high-growth areas, alongside persistent demand for cybersecurity and
data expertise. Collectively, the literature underscores the urgency of continuous upskilling,
organizational redesign toward leaner, outc ome-focused teams, and proactive governance
frameworks to convert AI -driven transformation into sustainable opportunities for IT
professionals rather than widespread displacement.
4. CONCEPTUAL BACKGROUND AND THEORETICAL FOUNDATIONS
AI in the IT context re fers to systems capable of performing tasks that traditionally required
human cognitive effort —pattern recognition, content generation, decision support, and
increasingly autonomous action via agents. Generative AI accelerates coding, documentation,
and tr oubleshooting; agentic AI can execute multi -step workflows with reduced human
intervention.
Relevant theoretical lenses include:
 Task-based approach to technological change: AI substitutes for routine, codifiable
tasks while complementing non -routine, judgment-intensive ones (building on Autor
and others).
 Human-machine collaboration / collective intelligence : Emphasis shifts from
“humans vs. machines” to “humans and machines” working synergistically (Malone
and related MIT perspectives). Humans contribute creativity, ethical judgment,
contextual understanding, and oversight; AI contributes speed, scale, and pattern
detection.
 Socio-technical systems theory : Technology, people, processes, and organizational
structure co-evolve. AI alters team structures towa rd leaner, more integrated models
focused on outcomes rather than processes.
 Skill-biased and routine -biased technological change , updated for the AI era:
Demand rises for high -level cognitive, social, and adaptive skills alongside AI
literacy.
5. IMPACT OF AI ON IT ROLES: TRANSFORMATION DYNAMICS
AI affects IT roles along three dimensions: positive (productivity and capability gains),
negative (displacement risks for certain tasks or junior positions), and transformative
(redefinition of responsibilities).
Most affected areas:
5.1 Software development / engineering:
 Shift from writing code line -by-line to directing coding agents, reviewing AI -generated

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 259

output, system architecture, integration, and ensuring production readiness. Junior/entry -level
coding tasks face higher automation exposure; senior roles emphasize judgment, design, and
orchestration. Engineering roles have shown relative resilience in hiring data despite broader
market fluctuations.
5.2 IT support and operations:
 Routine ticket resolution, monitoring, and basic troubleshooting are increasingly handled by
AI agents, freeing humans for complex escalation, root -cause analysis, and customer
empathy.
5.3 Data-related roles:
Heightened technical barriers and demand for advanced analytics, ML opera tions, and
interpretation of AI outputs. Data scientists and related positions project strong growth (e.g.,
BLS projections of ~33% for data scientists through 2034).
5.4 IT management and architecture:
 Move toward orchestration of human -AI teams, FinOps for AI workloads, decision
architecture, and governance. Transparency and accountability become central.
5.5 Cybersecurity and identity/access management:
 Expanded scope to secure AI systems, agents, LLMs (e.g., against jailbreaks), and manage
agent identities an d permissions. Strong projected growth (information security analysts
~28% in some BLS horizons).
6. CHALLENGES AND RISKS
 Displacement and inequality: Greater risk for routine/junior tasks and workers lacking
access to upskilling. Potential “two-track” labor market.
 Skills obsolescence and continuous learning pressure : Rapid change demands
ongoing investment.
 Ethical, bias, privacy, and accountability issues : Need for transparent AI systems
and human oversight.
 Over-reliance and deskilling : Risk of eroding foundational skills if AI is used
without understanding.
 Organizational inertia and talent shortages : Difficulty finding or developing
hybrid talent; cybersecurity and AI ethics gaps are acute.
 Entry-level pipeline disruption : Reduced traditional j unior roles may hinder long -
term talent development.
7. OPPORTUNITIES AND STRATEGIC RECOMMENDATIONS
Artificial Intelligence creates a dual landscape of risk and opportunity for IT employees and
the organizations that employ them. While routine and highly c odifiable tasks face
automation pressure, the same technology opens pathways for higher -value work, accelerated
career progression, and organizational transformation. Realizing these opportunities requires
deliberate action at three levels: the individual professional, the employing organization, and
the broader education and policy ecosystem.
The following recommendations are grounded in current industry evidence on skills
evolution, productivity gains, and emerging role structures.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 260

7.1 For IT employees:
 Cultivate AI fluency layered onto existing expertise rather than starting over.
 Develop orchestration, critical evaluation of AI outputs, business acumen, and soft
skills.
 Seek roles involving integration, governance, or human-AI design.
 Practice continuou s learning (certifications, projects with AI tools, cross -functional
exposure).
7.2 For organizations:
 Redesign work around outcomes and human-AI collaboration before resizing teams.
 Invest heavily in upskilling (AI literacy, governance, architecture) and create internal
pathways.
 Introduce new roles and flatten structures while maintaining human oversight.
 Embed responsible AI practices (audits, ethics training, clear accountability).
 Measure success via productivity, innovation, employee experience, and e thical
metrics—not solely headcount reduction.
7.3 For educators and policymakers:
 Update curricula with AI engineering, ethics, and hybrid skills.
 Support accessible reskilling programs and address equity in AI access.
 Encourage public-private collaboration on workforce transitions.
8. SKILLS TRANSFORMATION – GREATER DETAIL
Technical skills in highest demand include:
 Practical use of coding agents and generative tools
 Agentic workflow design and multi-agent orchestration
 LLM application patterns (RAG, evaluation, guardrails)
 AI security (including model and agent security)
 Data platforms and observability for AI systems
 Cost and performance management of AI infrastructure
Differentiating human skills that become more valuable as routine work is automated:
 Critical evaluation and verification of AI outputs
 Systems thinking and architectural judgment
 Ethical reasoning and responsible AI practice
 Business-domain translation and stakeholder communication
 Change leadership and the ability to redesign work processes
Evidence indicates that skills in AI -exposed roles change significantly faster than in less -
exposed roles. Entry -level positions that remain open increasingly demand capabilities
traditionally associated with more senior staff (judgment, lead ership, integration).
Continuous, problem -based learning embedded in real work contexts is therefore more
effective than one-off training events.
9. DISCUSSION CONCEPTUAL ANALYSIS
The conceptual analysis reveals that Artificial Intelligence is not merely an incremental tool
within the IT function but a structural force that reconfigures the nature of work, the
distribution of tasks between humans and machines, and the competencies t hat confer value.
The evidence synthesized in this study supports a nuanced interpretation that moves beyond
binary narratives of mass displacement versus seamless augmentation. Instead, AI produces

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 261

differentiated effects across roles, experience levels, a nd organizational contexts.Routine,
high-volume, and highly codifiable activities —boilerplate coding, basic ticket resolution,
repetitive monitoring, and standard documentation —are increasingly absorbed by generative
and agentic systems. This creates measu rable productivity gains and frees capacity, yet it
simultaneously compresses traditional entry points into IT careers. Junior professionals who
previously developed foundational skills through such tasks now face a steeper requirement
for judgment, verifi cation, and integration abilities earlier in their trajectories. Without
intentional redesign of apprenticeship and learning models, organizations risk eroding the
pipeline that produces future senior talent.
Ethical and socio -technical dimensions further  shape the trajectory. Issues of transparency,
bias, data privacy, accountability for agent actions, and the psychological experience of
working alongside AI systems cannot be treated as secondary. Trust, both in the technology
and in organizational proces ses, becomes a critical enabling condition. Equity considerations
are equally salient: differential access to high -quality AI tools and training risks creating
polarized outcomes within the IT workforce and across regions.Overall, the discussion
underscores that the future of IT employees will be determined less by the inherent
capabilities of AI and more by the adaptive responses of individuals, organizations, and
supporting institutions. Conceptual clarity around the continuum from automation to
orchestration, the layered nature of required capabilities, and the temporal distinction
between short-term efficiency gains and longer -term capability formation provides a useful
foundation for navigating this transition. At the same time, the demand for higher -order
human contributions intensifies. Orchestration of AI agents, critical evaluation of outputs,
architectural coherence, ethical oversight, and translation of technical capability into business
outcomes emerge as differentiating activities.
10. Findings / Key Insights
These findings collectively indicate that the future of IT employees centers on elevated
human contribution within hybrid intelligent systems rather than wholesale replacement.
Conceptual and practical attention to the orchestration -judgment continuum, capability
layering, and enabling organizational and institutional conditions offers the most constructive
path forward. So there are different key insights that are
10.1 Differentiated rather than uniform impact
AI affects IT roles unevenly. Softw are development, data -related work, IT
support/operations, and management/architecture experience the strongest transformation.
Productivity gains are widespread, while displacement risks concentrate on routine and
junior-level tasks. Transformative effect s include role redefinition toward oversight,
integration, and governance.
10.2 Shift from execution to orchestration and judgment
The core movement is from hands -on production of code, tickets, and reports toward
directing AI agents, evaluating outputs, e nsuring system coherence, and exercising ethical
and business judgment. Human value increasingly resides in the layers of work that AI
cannot yet reliably perform alone.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 262

10.3 Skills evolution is rapid and hybrid
Technical AI fluency (agent orchestration, evaluation of generative outputs, AI security and
infrastructure) is necessary but insufficient. Differentiating capabilities include critical
thinking, systems architecture, domain/business acumen, communication, and responsible AI
practice. Skills in AI-exposed roles change significantly faster than in less-exposed ones.
10.4 Entry-level and career-ladder pressures
Automation of traditional junior tasks can remove learning opportunities. Without redesigned
apprenticeship models that emphasize verification, ind ependent diagnosis, and guided use of
AI as a tutor, the talent pipeline for experienced professionals may weaken over time.
10.5 Organizational design is a decisive mediator
Leaner, outcome -focused IT structures and human -agent teams deliver advantages when
accompanied by heavy upskilling, clear accountability frameworks, and expanded success
metrics (productivity, innovation, employee experience, ethical performance). Pure
headcount reduction without these elements yields suboptimal and potentially fragile results.
10.6 Emerging roles and opportunity creation
New and expanded positions —AI architects, integrators, agent engineers, governance and
ethics specialists, human-AI collaboration designers, and forward-deployed hybrid roles—are
growing. Professionals who layer AI capability onto existing expertise and demonstrate
measurable impact are positioned to capture wage and career premiums.
11. CONCLUSION
Artificial Intelligence is reshaping the IT profession more profoundly than any previous wave
of technology. This conceptual study shows that the impact is neither pure job destruction nor
automatic enrichment of every role. Instead, AI is driving a structural shift along a
continuum—from the automation of routine tasks, through human –AI collaboration, toward
work centered on orchestration, critical judgment, ethical oversight, and the creation of
business value.
Routine coding, basic support, monitoring, and repetitive analytical work are increasingly
handled by generative and agentic systems. This produces clear p roductivity gains and frees
capacity, yet it also compresses traditional entry -level learning pathways. At the same time,
demand is rising for professionals who can design, direct, evaluate, and govern AI systems;
integrate them into complex enterprise env ironments; and exercise the uniquely human
capacities of contextual understanding, ethical reasoning, creativity, and accountability. New
and expanding roles —AI architects, agent orchestrators, governance specialists, human -AI
collaboration designers, and hybrid technical -business profiles —reflect this elevation of
work. In the final analysis, the future of IT employees will be defined less by what AI can do
and more by how deliberately humans choose to work with it. Those who adapt with
intention are posit ioned not only to remain relevant but to shape the next generation of
technology work.

AI as a Supporting Tool: An Empirical Study of Human–AI Collaboration and
Employee Productivity
Ms M.Shobana
 Assistant Professor
Valliammal College for Women Chennai.
Ms.N.D.Harini
MBA Student, Valliammal College for Women Chennai.
Abstract
Artificial Intelligence (AI) is increasingly becoming an important supporting technology in
the modern workplace. Organizations are adopting AI -based tools to assist employees in
information processing, content generation, data analysis, routine t ask completion, and
decision support. However, the effectiveness of AI depends not only on its technological
capabilities but also on its reliability and the manner in which employees collaborate with AI
systems. This study examines the relationship betwee n AI capability, AI reliability, human –
AI collaboration, and employee productivity. A descriptive and analytical research design is
adopted, and primary data are collected from employees who use AI -based tools in their
workplace through a structured questi onnaire. The study measures four constructs: AI
Capability, AI Reliability, Human –AI Collaboration, and Employee Productivity using a
five-point Likert scale. Data are analysed using SPSS through frequency and percentage
analysis, mean and standard deviati on, Cronbach’s alpha, Chi -square analysis, and Pearson
correlation. The study examines whether AI capability and AI reliability are significantly
related to human –AI collaboration and whether human –AI collaboration is significantly
related to employee productivity. The study contributes to the growing discussion on human -
centred AI adoption by examining AI primarily as a supporting tool rather than only as a
mechanism for replacing human work.
Keywords: Artificial Intelligence, AI Capability, AI Reliability , Human –AI Collaboration,
Employee Productivity, Workplace AI, AI-Supported Work
 Introduction
Artificial Intelligence (AI) has become an important part of the modern workplace.
Organizations use AI for activities such as information processing, customer s ervice, data
analysis, content creation, forecasting, automation, and decision support. With the growth of
generative AI, employees are increasingly using AI tools in their daily work.  AI can perform
repetitive tasks quickly, process large amounts of infor mation, identify patterns, and provide
suggestions. This can reduce the time spent on routine activities and allow employees to
focus on tasks that require human knowledge and judgment. AI can therefore be used not
only to replace certain tasks but also to  support and complement employees  (Raisch &
Krakowski, 2021).  The effectiveness of AI depends on its capability and reliability . AI
capability refers to the ability of AI tools to perform workplace tasks such as processing

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 265

information, generating content, and supporting routine activities. AI reliability refers to the
accuracy, consistency, and dependability of AI-generated outputs.
Human–AI collaboration focuses on combining the strengths of AI with human knowledge,
experience, creativity, and decision-making. Jarrahi (2018) highlights the complementary role
of humans and AI in organizational decision-making. Similarly, Shrestha et al. (2019) explain
that AI can influence how responsibilities are shared between humans and machines.  AI may
also contribute to  employee productivity  by reducing repetitive work, speeding up
information processing, and helping employees complete tasks more efficiently. Therefore,
this study examines the relationship between AI capability, AI reliability, human –AI
collaboration, and employee productivity in the workplace.
2 Review of Literature
2.1 AI Capability
AI capability refers to the extent to which an AI system can perform or support workplace
activities. AI systems can process information, identify patterns, generate content , summarize
information, and automate repetitive activities. Davenport and Ronanki (2018) identify
several practical organizational applications of AI, including process automation, cognitive
insights, and engagement.  From an employee perspective, greater AI capability can provide
more opportunities to delegate routine activities and obtain rapid information. AI capability
may therefore facilitate greater interaction between employees and AI systems.
2.2 AI Reliability
AI reliability concerns the consistenc y, accuracy, dependability, and trustworthiness of AI -
generated outputs. Employees may be less willing to incorporate AI into their work if they
frequently encounter inaccurate or inconsistent outputs. Conversely, reliable AI systems may
encourage employee s to use AI more extensively as a workplace support mechanism.
Reliability is particularly important because AI -generated outputs may require human
verification. Therefore, employees' perceptions of reliability may influence the effectiveness
of human–AI interaction.
2.3 Human–AI Collaboration
Human–AI collaboration refers to situations in which employees and AI systems contribute
to workplace activities according to their respective capabilities. AI can provide
computational speed, information processing, pattern recognition, and automation, while
employees contribute contextual understanding, professional judgment, creativity,
communication, and accountability.  Raisch and Krakowski (2021) describe the relationship
between automation and augmentation as an important organizational issue. The
collaborative approach emphasizes augmentation, in which technology supports rather than
completely substitutes human activities.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 266

2.4 Employee Productivity
Employee productivity refers to employees' ability to complete work effectively and
efficiently. In AI-supported workplaces, productivity may be reflected in reduced time spent
on routine activities, improved task completion, greater work efficiency, and t he ability to
concentrate on higher -value activities. Brynjolfsson et al. (2023) provide evidence that
generative AI can influence worker productivity in customer -support work, although
productivity effects can vary among employees and tasks.
2.5 Research Gap
Existing literature has examined AI adoption, automation, augmentation, human –AI
interaction, and the future of work from several perspectives. However, there remains a need
for empirical examination of AI as a supporting workplace tool by jointly cons idering AI
capability, AI reliability, human–AI collaboration, and employee productivity.
The present study addresses this gap by examining the relationships among these four
constructs using primary data from employees who use AI-based tools in their workplace.
3. Objectives of the Study
1. To examine the relationship between AI capability and human –AI collaboration
among employees.
2. To examine the relationship between AI reliability and human –AI collaboration
among employees.
3. To examine the relationship between human–AI collaboration and employee
productivity.
4. To examine the relationship between AI capability, AI reliability, and employee
productivity.
3.1 Hypotheses of the Study
H1: There is a significant relationship between AI capability and human –AI collaboration
among employees.
H2: There is a significant relationship between AI reliability and human –AI collaboration
among employees.
H3: There is a significant relationship between human –AI collaboration and employee
productivity.
H4: There is a significant relationship between AI capability and employee productivity.
H5: There is a significant relationship between AI reliability and employee productivity.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 267

4. Research Methodology
The study adopts a descriptive and analytical research design  to examine AI as a
supporting tool in the workplace. The study focuses on four constructs: AI Capability, AI
Reliability, Human–AI Collaboration, and Employee Productivity. Primary data are collected
from employees who use AI -based tools for workplace-related activities through a structured
questionnaire. The questionnaire consists of demographic questions and statements
measuring the four constructs using a five -point Likert scale ranging from 1 = Strongly
Disagree to 5 = Strongly Agree. Purposive samplin g is used to identify respondents with
experience using AI tools in their workplace. The collected data are coded and analysed using
SPSS. Frequency and percentage analysis are used to describe the demographic
characteristics of respondents. Mean and stand ard deviation are used for descriptive analysis,
while Cronbach’s alpha is used to assess the reliability of the measurement scales. Chi -square
analysis is used to examine associations between selected categorical variables. Pearson
correlation is used to examine relationships among AI capability, AI reliability, human –AI
collaboration, and employee productivity. All hypotheses are tested at the 5% level of
significance.

5.  Managerial Implications
The study provides several implications for managers and organizations adopting AI
technologies. First, organizations should evaluate AI systems based not only on their
technological sophistication but also on their usefulness to employees. AI tools should be
selected according to the actual requirements of workplace tasks.  Second, relia bility is
important for effective AI adoption. Organizations should establish appropriate procedures
for checking AI -generated information, particularly when employees use AI for important
workplace decisions. Third, managers should encourage employees to use AI as a supporting
tool while maintaining appropriate human judgment. Employees should be encouraged to
evaluate AI outputs rather than accepting them without verification.  Fourth, organizations
should provide employees with appropriate AI -related training. Training can help employees
understand how AI systems work, how to use them effectively, and how to identify potential
limitations in AI -generated outputs.  Finally, organizations should focus on improving
employee productivity through human–AI collab oration rather than measuring AI
success only through automation or workforce reduction.
6. Conclusion
Artificial Intelligence is increasingly becoming part of everyday workplace activities. The
present study examines AI from a supporting -tool perspective and  focuses on the
relationships among AI capability, AI reliability, human –AI collaboration, and employee
productivity. The proposed framework recognizes that AI and human employees can
contribute different capabilities to workplace activities. AI can provid e speed, information
processing, automation, and analytical support, while employees contribute professional
knowledge, contextual understanding, judgment, creativity, and accountability. The empirical
analysis using descriptive statistics, reliability ana lysis, Chi-square, and Pearson correlation
provides a simple framework for examining these relationships. The study particularly
focuses on whether AI capability and reliability are associated with human –AI collaboration
and whether such collaboration is associated with employee productivity.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 271

The study therefore contributes to the growing discussion of human -centred AI adoption by
emphasizing AI as a supporting workplace technology. Effective AI adoption requires not
only capable and reliable technology but  also appropriate human involvement and
responsible use.
7.  Limitations and Scope for Future Research
The study has certain limitations. First, the study is based on cross -sectional primary data,
which limits the ability to establish causal relationships. Se cond, the study relies on
respondents' perceptions of AI capability, reliability, collaboration, and productivity. Third,
AI technologies are developing rapidly, and perceptions of AI may change as employees gain
greater experience with these technologies.  Future studies may examine the model across
different industries and occupational groups. Longitudinal studies may also be undertaken to
examine how human –AI collaboration changes over time. Future researchers may
additionally examine variables such as AI  literacy, employee trust, job satisfaction,
innovation, and organizational support.

The Paradox of Progress: A Study on AI in Daily Business Operations — With
Reference to Small Scale Business
Dr.V.Nithya Priyadharshini
Associate Professor
Department of Business Administation
Valliamal College for Women

Bharathi P
2nd MBA
Department of Business Administation
Valliamal College for Women
Abstract
Artificial Intelligence (AI) has moved rapidly from a specialised technology into a routine
feature of everyday business operations, promising faster decision -making, lower co sts, and
higher productivity. Yet this promise sits alongside a paradox: the very technology designed
to simplify operations often introduces new layers of complexity, cost, and dependency,
particularly for small scale businesses operating with limited cap ital, technical skill, and
infrastructure. This paper examines how AI is being adopted and experienced in the daily
operations of small scale businesses, and explores the gap between the theoretical potential of
AI and its practical, on -ground impact. Draw ing on existing literature and primary data
collected from small business owners and employees, the study investigates adoption
patterns, common use -cases, perceived benefits, and the operational, financial, and
psychological barriers that together shape t his "paradox of progress." Findings indicate that
while AI offers measurable gains in efficiency and customer engagement, uneven access to
resources, persistent skill gaps, and trust deficits continue to limit its full realisation among
small enterprises. The paper closes with practical implications for owners, technology
providers, and policymakers seeking more inclusive AI adoption.
Keywords Artificial Intelligence; Small Scale Business; Business Operations; Digital
Transformation; Technology Adoption; Pr oductivity Paradox; Operational Efficiency;
Automation; AI Adoption Barriers; SMEs; Decision -Making; Cost Reduction; Customer
Engagement; Digital Divide
1. Introduction
Over the last decade, Artificial Intelligence has shifted from an emerging research fie ld into a
practical tool embedded in everyday business functions such as accounting, inventory
management, customer service, marketing, and decision -making. Cloud -based AI tools,
chatbots, automated billing systems, and predictive analytics dashboards are no longer
reserved for large corporations; they are increasingly marketed to small and micro businesses
as affordable, plug -and-play solutions. This growing accessibility has fuelled optimism that

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 273

AI can act as a great equaliser, allowing small businesses to compete with larger, resource -
rich organisations by automating repetitive tasks, reducing manual error, and freeing owners
to focus on strategic growth.  However, this optimism exists alongside a quieter, less -
discussed reality — what this paper refers t o as the "paradox of progress." The same
technologies that promise efficiency can, for a small business owner without a dedicated IT
team, translate into new costs, unfamiliar workflows, data -privacy concerns, and a learning
curve that eats into the very time and resources AI is meant to save. Progress, in this sense, is
not evenly distributed: it depends on a business's existing digital literacy, financial cushion,
and willingness to experiment. This paper studies this paradox specifically in the context o f
small scale businesses, examining how AI is actually used in daily operations, what benefits
and frictions it creates, and what this means for the future of inclusive technology adoption.
2. Review of Literature
2.1 Bolfek, Rajko & Bolfek (2026)
Using su rvey data from 228 SMEs analysed through multiple linear regression, this study
found that AI applications had a statistically significant positive effect on labour productivity
and on reducing operating costs, while their effect on business process effici ency was more
moderate and dependent on how AI was applied. In comparison, the present paper narrows
this large-sample, quantitative picture down to the lived, day-to-day experience of small scale
businesses, asking not just whether productivity improves o n average, but how owners and
employees actually experience that change on the ground.
2.2 D'Amico, Audretsch & Belitski (2025)
Applying the Technology –Organisation–Environment (TOE) framework, this study showed
that a small business's internal R&D investm ent, digital infrastructure, and external
knowledge networks jointly shape its propensity to adopt AI. This paper draws on the same
TOE logic to structure its methodology, but shifts the unit of analysis from adoption
propensity to adoption experience — treating readiness factors as explanations for the
barriers observed in the results rather than as the primary outcome of interest.
2.3 Brynjolfsson, Rock & Syverson (2017)
This influential NBER working paper coined the modern framing of the "AI productivity
paradox," arguing that a mismatch between optimistic expectations and disappointing
aggregate statistics reflects implementation lags, intangible complementary investment s, and
mismeasurement rather than an absence of real value. The present paper borrows this paradox
framing directly for its title and central argument but relocates it from the macroeconomic
level to the micro level of individual small businesses, where th e same tension between
expectation and lived reality plays out in daily operational decisions.
2.4 Yesuf, Fields, Jain & Kassa (2025)
This bibliometric and systematic review, guided by the PRISMA 2021 protocol, mapped the
growing body of literature on AI's  role in SME innovation and competitiveness, and

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 274

highlighted a shortage of primary, context -specific studies compared to broad conceptual
reviews. This paper responds directly to that identified gap by contributing primary, field -
level findings on small business operations rather than a further review of secondary sources.
2.5 Malik (2025)
Surveying 150 SMEs, this study found that financial constraints, limited technical expertise,
and general scepticism toward new technology were the dominant obstacles to AI adoption,
though many SMEs offset these obstacles through low -cost AI tools and external
partnerships. This paper's barrier -related findings align closely with this earlier work, but
extend it by connecting specific barriers to specific daily operationa l functions — showing
where in the business the paradox is felt most acutely.
3. Methodology
This study adopts a descriptive research design combining quantitative and qualitative
elements to capture both the extent and the texture of AI adoption in small scale businesses.
A purposive sample of small business owners, managers, and employees across a range of
sectors — retail, food service, professional services, and local manufacturing — was selected
on the basis that the business currently uses, or has act ively considered, at least one AI -
enabled tool in its daily operations. This sampling approach was chosen to ensure that
respondents could speak from direct experience rather than general perception.  Primary data
was collected through a structured question naire supplemented by short semi -structured
interviews with a subset of respondents, allowing numerical patterns from the survey to be
cross-checked against richer, first -hand accounts of how AI tools are actually used, adapted,
or abandoned in practice. The questionnaire covered four broad areas: the type and frequency
of AI tool usage, perceived changes in efficiency and cost, barriers encountered during
adoption, and overall satisfaction or regret regarding the decision to adopt. Secondary data
drawn from existing academic literature and industry reports was used to contextualise and
validate the primary findings.  Data from the structured questionnaire was analysed using
descriptive statistics — frequencies, percentages, and simple cross -tabulations — to identify
overall adoption patterns, while interview responses were analysed thematically to surface
recurring language around trust, workload, and value. The study is subject to the usual
limitations of small -sample, cross-sectional research: findings refl ect a snapshot in time and
the specific businesses sampled, and causal claims about AI's impact on performance should
be read as indicative rather than definitive.
4. Result and Discussion
4.1 Extent and Pace of AI Adoption
The findings show that AI adopti on among small scale businesses is real but uneven, with
most respondents using at least one AI -enabled tool, yet very few using it as part of an
integrated, deliberate strategy. Adoption in most cases began informally — an owner trying a
free chatbot plug in or an accounting app with a built -in AI feature — rather than through
planned investment.

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 275

This pattern is consistent with the gradual, bottom -up adoption curve described in the
literature, and supports the view that small businesses treat AI less as a s trategic
transformation and more as an incremental upgrade to existing tools. The pace of adoption
appears to accelerate once an initial tool demonstrates a small, visible win, suggesting that
early, low -risk successes matter more to small businesses than the scale of any single AI
investment.
4.2 Common AI Applications in Daily Operations
The most frequently reported use -cases were customer -facing chatbots, automated invoicing
and bookkeeping, inventory or stock -level prediction, and AI -assisted content creation for
marketing and social media. These functions share a common trait: they are repetitive, rule -
based, and relatively low -risk if the AI output requires minor correction.  This concentration
of use-cases indicates that small businesses gravitate to ward AI applications that offer quick,
tangible relief from routine administrative burden rather than toward more complex
applications such as predictive analytics for strategic decision -making, which remain largely
confined to larger enterprises with dedicated data resources.
4.3 Impact on Operational Efficiency and Time Management
A majority of respondents reported measurable time savings on routine tasks after adopting
AI tools, particularly in customer response times and administrative record -keeping. Several
owners described being able to redirect saved time toward business development activities
they had previously postponed.  These efficiency gains, however, were rarely immediate;
most respondents described an initial period of slower operations while staff adjusted to new
workflows. This finding echoes the broader productivity paradox literature, which attributes
early-stage productivity dips to the learning curve and reorganisation costs that precede any
net efficiency gain.
4.4 Cost Implications: Savings versus New Expenditure
While many respondents reported reduced labour costs on routine tasks, a similar number
reported unexpected costs related to subscription fees, software integration, and, in a few
cases, paid training or external consultants bro ught in to set up the tools correctly.This mixed
cost picture reinforces the paradox central to this paper: AI is frequently marketed as a low -
cost solution for small businesses, but the total cost of adoption — including hidden costs of
time, training, an d troubleshooting — is often underestimated at the point of purchase,
muting the net financial benefit in the short term.
4.5 Barriers to Adoption: Skill, Awareness, and Trust
The most commonly cited barriers were a lack of technical skill within the busin ess, limited
awareness of which tools were appropriate for their specific needs, and a general distrust of
AI-generated outputs, particularly around accuracy and data privacy. Owners without prior
digital experience were considerably more likely to describ e AI adoption as "trial and error."
These barriers were rarely experienced in isolation; a lack of technical skill often deepened
distrust, since respondents were unable to verify or troubleshoot AI outputs themselves. This

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 276

compounding effect suggests that  barrier-reduction strategies aimed at only one obstacle —
cost subsidies alone, for instance — are unlikely to be sufficient without accompanying skill -
building support.
4.6 Employee Perception and Workplace Adjustment
Employee reactions to AI adoption ra nged from enthusiasm among younger, more digitally
comfortable staff to visible anxiety among longer-tenured employees who associated AI tools
with a threat to their role. Business owners who involved employees early in tool selection
reported smoother ado ption than those who introduced AI tools unilaterally.  This finding
highlights that the paradox of progress is not purely technical or financial but also
psychological: the same tool can be experienced as empowering or threatening depending on
how its intr oduction is managed internally, underscoring the importance of change -
management practices even at small business scale.
4.7 Customer Experience and Engagement Outcomes
Businesses using AI -enabled customer service tools generally reported faster response t imes
and improved availability outside normal business hours, which several respondents linked to
modest increases in customer retention and enquiry -to-sale conversion.At the same time, a
notable minority of respondents described customer complaints about impersonal or
repetitive chatbot responses, indicating that AI's benefits to customer experience are
conditional on careful configuration and are not guaranteed simply by adopting the tool.
4.8 The Paradox of Progress: Reconciling Promise and Practice
Taken together, the findings confirm that AI does deliver genuine operational benefits to
small scale businesses, but that these benefits are neither immediate nor evenly distributed,
and are frequently offset by hidden costs, skill gaps, and internal resi stance. Progress, in this
setting, is real but conditional.  This conditionality is the essence of the paradox explored in
this paper: AI is simultaneously a tool of empowerment and a source of new friction for small
businesses, and its net impact depends l ess on the sophistication of the technology itself than
on the resources, skills, and change-management capacity a business brings to its adoption.
5. Conclusion
This study set out to examine the paradox of progress surrounding AI adoption in the daily
operations of small scale businesses, and finds that the technology's promise of efficiency,
cost reduction, and improved customer engagement is genuine but only partially and
unevenly realised in practice, constrained by financial, technical, and human barri ers that
mirror those identified in the wider literature. For small business owners, the practical
implication is that successful AI adoption depends as much on preparation, staff involvement,
and realistic cost expectations as on the choice of tool itself ; for technology providers and
policymakers, the findings point to a need for more accessible training, transparent pricing,
and support structures that can narrow the gap between AI's theoretical potential and its
everyday reality for the small businesses that make up the backbone of most economies.

A STUDY ON THE IMPACT OF HUSTLE SOCIAL CULTURE ON EMPLOYEES
WORK-LIFE BALANCE AT CHENNAI

RAJESHWARI R
Assistant Professor
Department of Business Administration
Valliammal College for Women
KAVIYARASI M
II MBA
Department of  Business Administration
Valliammal College for Women
ABSTRACT
The modern workplace is becoming increasingly competitive and performance -oriented,
leading to greater expectations for productivity, long working hours, conti nuous work
involvement, and professional commitment. This has contributed to the emergence of hustle
social culture, where constant availability, high performance, and personal sacrifice are often
associated with professional success. This study examines t he impact of hustle social culture
on employees’ work -life balance in Chennai, with a focus on factors such as extended
working hours, workload, performance pressure, digital connectivity, and workplace
expectations. It explores how these factors influence  employees’ ability to manage their
professional responsibilities along with family, social, and personal commitments. The study
also highlights the importance of sustainable work practices, healthy work boundaries,
effective workload management, and emplo yee well -being initiatives. The findings are
expected to provide useful insights for organizations and HR professionals in creating
supportive workplace environments that promote both employee well -being and
organizational productivity.
Keywords: Hustle So cial Culture, Work -Life Balance, Working Hours, Workload,
Performance Pressure, Employee Well -being, Workplace Culture, Organizational Support,
Chennai.
INTRODUCTION
The present work environment is increasingly influenced by competition, performance
expectations, technological development, and changing work practices. Employees are often
expected to complete tasks within strict deadlines, achieve targets, and remain connected to
their work beyond regular working hours. These conditions have contribute d to the growth of
hustle social culture, where long working hours, continuous productivity, and strong career
commitment are often associated with professional success.  Hustle culture can encourage
employees to work hard and achieve their career goals. Ho wever, excessive involvement in
work may affect the time available for family, social activities, rest, and personal interests.
This creates challenges in maintaining work -life balance, which refers to the ability of
employees to manage their professional responsibilities along with their personal lives.  The
increasing use of smartphones, emails, and digital communication has also made it easier for
employees to remain connected to work after office hours. As a result, the boundary between

National Level Seminar on Application of Artificial Intelligence in
Commerce and Management
ISBN: 978-81-180170-6-6                       DOI:10.65647/msp/141/2026 Page 279

working time and personal time may become less clear. Continuous workload and
performance pressure may further contribute to stress, fatigue, and reduced personal time.
In the context of Chennai, where employees work across various sectors and professional
environments, un derstanding these changing work patterns is important. The present study
examines the impact of hustle social culture on employees’ work -life balance, with particular
focus on working hours, workload, performance pressure, and constant work connectivity.
The study aims to understand the challenges faced by employees and highlight the
importance of creating a work environment that supports both organizational performance
and employee well-being.
OBJECTIVES OF THE STUDY
PRIMARY OBJECTIVE
1. To study the impact of hustle social culture on employees’ work -life balance by
collecting responses from 400 employees across 20 IT companies through Google
Forms.
SECONDARY OBJECTIVE
1. To examine the impact of burnout caused by high -performance expectations and
workplace pres sure on employees’ personal life satisfaction as part of work -life
balance.
2. To examine the effect of hustle culture on job satisfaction and work performance
3. To study the relationship between productivity pressure and different levels of job
satisfaction among employees.
REVIEW OF LITERATURE
Regmi, R., & Manandhar, S. (2025). Hustle Culture and Workplace Anxiety: The
Psychological Effect of Overworked Behaviour among Working Individuals. DAV
Research Journal, 4(1), 18 -33, The study found that excessive work commitment and
performance pressure can increase workplace anxiety and disturb work -life balance. It
highlights the importance of mental health support and maintaining a healthy work -life
balance.
Mosha, A. (2025). Work-Life Balance: A Woman’s Agency Hustl e? Journal of
Management and Science, 15(3), 101 –117. This study examines work -life balance among
employed women in Tanzania. It highlights the challenges caused by gender roles, domestic
responsibilities, and lack of workplace support. The study emphasizes flexible work
arrangements and supportive systems to reduce stress and improve employee well-being.
George, A.S. (2024). “The ‘Anti -Hustle’ Ethos Among Generation Z Workers: An
Investigation into Shifting Attitudes Towards Work -Life Balance.” PU IRP, 2(5), 96 –
109. This study examines the anti -hustle mindset among Generation Z workers and their
preference for work -life balance, flexibility, and meaningful work. It highlights factors such
as workplace fatigue, job dissatisfaction, and personal inter ests. The study suggests that
flexible policies and supportive communication can promote healthier work practices.
Arunadevi, M. V., Rajalakshmi, M. K., & Subasini, M. (2024). Harmony in the Hustle:
Nurturing Mental Well -Being for Sustainable Work -Life Balance, II, 167–172. This study

examines the relationship between mental well -being and work -life balance in high -pressure
work environments. It highlights the importance of mental wellness initiatives, supportive
workplaces, and stress management in reducin g burnout and promoting sustainable
performance.
RESEARCH METHODOLOGY
Research Design
The study adopts a Descriptive research design to examine the impact of hustle social culture
on employees’ work-life balance
Data Collection
The study uses primary and secondary data.
Primary Data: Collected directly from employees through a structured questionnaire.
Secondary Data : Collected from journals, research articles, books, websites, and other
relevant sources.
Sampling Technique & Sample Size
Convenience sampling technique is used to select respondents who are easily accessible to
the researcher. The study covers 20 selected IT companies, with 20 employees selected from
each company. Therefore, the total sample size of the study is 400 employees.
Sample Size Calculation:
Convenience sampling is used to select respondents who are easily accessible to the
researcher.
Sample Area
The study covers employees working in selected IT industries in Chennai
Statistical Tools Used
The collected data are analyzed using appropriate statistical tools such as:
Correlation Analysis
ANOVA
Research Instrument
A structured questionnaire is used as the primary research instrument. The questionnaire
consists of questions related to hustle social culture and employees’ work-life balance.
Data Analysis Software:  The collected data is analyzed using SPSS (Statistical Package
for the Social Sciences) . It is used to organize the data and perform statistical tests such as
correlation and ANOVA to study the relationship between the variables.
DATA ANALYSIS AND INTERPRETATION
TOOLS APPLIED: PEARSON CORRELATION TEST
NEED FOR THE TEST
The Pearson Correlation test is used to analyze the relationship between burnout from  high -
performance expectations and pressure affecting personal life satisfact ion. This test helps
determine whether higher burnout from continuous performance demands has a significant
relationship with employees’ satisfaction in managing personal life.
NULL HYPOTHESIS (H0)
There is no statistically significant correlation between burnout from high -performance
expectations and pressure affecting personal life satisfaction.
ALTERNATIVE HYPOTHESIS (H1)
There is a statistically significant correlation between burnout from high -performance

FINDINGS
 From the Correlation analysis, it was found that there was a strong positive relationship
between burnout from high-performance expectations and pressure affecting personal life
satisfaction, as the corre lation coefficient (r = 0.738) and p -value (0.000) indicate a
statistically significant relationship. This shows that higher burnout is associated with
greater pressure on personal life.
 From the One-Way ANOVA analysis, it was found that there was a significant difference
in Productivity Pressure across different levels of Job Satisfaction, as the p -value (0.000)
was less than 0.05. This indicates that employees’ productivity pressure varies depending
on their level of job satisfaction.
SUGGESSIONS
 Organizations should continue strengthening work -life balance policies, such as
flexible scheduling and supportive leave policies, as many employees reported
satisfaction with these initiatives.
 To examine the impact of hustle culture on employees’ work -life balance and
understand how workplace pressure affects their personal and professional lives.
 To assess the effect of hustle culture on employees’ job satisfaction and work
performance, with focus on productivity, motivation, and workplace expectations.
 To analyse the relationship between productivity pressure and different levels of job
satisfaction among employees and understand how work pressure influences their
satisfaction.
CONCLUSION
The study concludes that hustle social culture has a significant inf luence on employees’
work-life balance. Long working hours, heavy workloads, and performance pressure can
increase stress, reduce personal time, and affect job satisfaction.  The impact may vary based
on workload, job roles, and workplace support. Therefore , organizations should promote
reasonable workloads, flexibility, healthy work boundaries, and employee support to
maintain both employee well-being and sustainable performance.
